#6957. Storage Platform Engineer at Supabase

Supabase runs millions of Postgres databases, and storage performance is now one of the biggest levers on how fast, reliable, and affordable they are. We're building a software-defined, NVMe-over-Fabrics storage platform underneath Supabase Postgres. Postgres stays unmodified, and the new layer delivers near-local-NVMe performance with the durability and flexibility of networked storage.

As the senior engineer driving storage, you'll own this effort end to end: the architecture and build-vs-buy decisions, integration into our compute platform, and operating the system in production.

Your first goal is raw Postgres I/O performance at scale. In the longer term, the layer you build will provide the block-level snapshots and copy-on-write clones behind database branching, fast restores, and instant resizes. Because it's software-defined, it will also travel with us as we expand beyond a single cloud provider into multi-cloud and colocated infrastructure.

This is a foundational, high-ownership role. You'll set the technical direction for storage at Supabase and help grow the team around it.

Lead the architecture of Supabase's NVMe-oF storage platform, including evaluating and deciding between adopting existing software-defined storage and building on SPDK

Integrate networked block storage into our Kubernetes-based compute platform through CSI drivers and custom controllers, covering provisioning, attach/detach, resizing, and migration

Make Postgres fast on networked storage by profiling and tuning the full I/O path, from fsync, WAL, and checkpoints through the kernel block layer, NVMe/TCP, and the network

Design for durability and availability, including replication or erasure coding, failure domains, failover, and data-integrity verification

Build I/O isolation and QoS so that noisy neighbors can't degrade other tenants

Set performance and reliability SLOs, and build the benchmarking and observability to hold the system to them

Design the layer for block-level snapshots and copy-on-write clones, laying the groundwork for database branching

Keep the platform portable across AWS, other clouds, and bare-metal/colo

Write design docs, mentor engineers, and work closely with the Compute, Branching, Postgres, and Multicloud teams

Participate in on-call rotations, incident response, and post-incident reviews

Resolve escalated support issues, while also identifying and resolving systemic issues across the platform

Have 7+ years of software engineering experience, including significant time building and operating storage systems in production

Have built or operated distributed or software-defined block storage (for example Ceph RBD, Lightbits, Simplyblock, Portworx, LINSTOR/DRBD, or an in-house system)

Have hands-on experience with NVMe/TCP or NVMe-oF, including targets, initiators, multipath, and failover behavior

Have a deep understanding of Linux storage internals: the block layer, io_uring, page cache, O_DIRECT and fsync semantics, filesystems, and device-mapper

Write strong systems code in Go, plus C, C++, or Rust

Measure performance rigorously with fio, pgbench, eBPF, and perf, and can explain latency down to its individual components

Understand durability tradeoffs between replication, erasure coding, and consistency under failure

Have led technical direction on a complex system: design docs, tradeoffs, and bringing others along

Have experience with Kubernetes controllers and/or CSI drivers

Have been on call for large production systems

Communicate clearly in writing and thrive in async, globally distributed teams

Experience implementing block-level snapshots, thin provisioning, or copy-on-write cloning

Postgres internals knowledge (WAL, buffer manager, checkpointer), or familiarity with OrioleDB

Experience with AWS networking and instance storage at scale (ENA, placement, bandwidth limits)

Experience running storage on bare metal or in colo

Contributions to open source storage projects or the Linux kernel storage stack

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#6954. Software Engineer - Postgres Tooling at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, Postgres Tooling to join our CLI team and help build the open-source tools developers use to change their Postgres schemas safely. You’ll join as a co-maintainer of pg-delta, our schema diff and migration engine, and grow into leading it.

Given two databases, or a database and a folder of SQL files describing the schema you want, pg-delta works out the differences and generates the migration to get from one to the other. It powers supabase db diff, db pull, and declarative schemas, and it runs underneath Supabase’s platform, branching, and AI agent workflows. Every migration it generates is verified on a throwaway clone before it’s trusted, and it’s tested against 331 scenarios on PostgreSQL 14–18. We’re now working toward a stable, standalone 1.0 release for any Postgres developer, on Supabase or not.

This is a correctness-first role. The SQL this tool generates runs against production databases, so privilege safety and avoiding data loss are non-negotiable. It’s a great fit for someone who loves Postgres at the catalog level, enjoys compiler-style problems, and thrives in async, fast-paced environments.

Co-maintain pg-delta: review and merge changes, including PRs written by AI agents, and grow into its lead maintainer.

Own correctness: triage bug reports from GitHub and support, fix diff and dependency-ordering bugs, and extend test coverage across Postgres versions.

Make sure generated migrations are privilege-safe and never silently lose data, by detecting destructive changes and rewriting them into safe equivalents where possible.

Help take pg-delta to a stable 1.0: a public API with a semver contract, documentation, and broader coverage of Postgres extensions.

Work with the open-source community: triage issues, review outside contributions, and say no gracefully when a request doesn’t fit.

Work with the teams across Supabase that build on pg-delta, so migrations are generated and run the same way everywhere.

Write the design docs and technical strategy that set the tool’s direction, working async across teams.

Share the CLI team’s on-call rotation. Weekdays are covered follow-the-sun, and because we ship packaged software that users can roll back, pages are rare.

Have 8+ years of experience as a software engineer, including deep, hands-on work with PostgreSQL.

Know Postgres internals at the catalog level: pg_catalog, pg_depend, object ownership, grants and default privileges, RLS policies, and extension membership.

Understand DDL and migration semantics: which changes take locks, which can’t run inside a transaction, how dependencies determine ordering, and how to spot a destructive change.

Treat a migration that silently loosens access as a top-severity bug.

Write strong TypeScript, and design tests for correctness, such as differential, property-based, or corpus testing across multiple Postgres versions.

Are comfortable with compiler-style problems: dependency graphs, topological ordering, cycle handling, and diffing.

Can confidently review complex changes in an intricate codebase, including AI-generated PRs.

Have owned software in production with users in the loop: staged rollouts, error monitoring, and triaging real bug reports.

Have led a technical project across several teams, from an ambiguous start to production.

Communicate clearly in writing and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Nice to have, roughly in order of how much they’d help:

Have built or maintained schema diff or migration tooling (such as migra, pg-schema-diff, Atlas, pgroll, sqitch, Prisma Migrate, or Flyway/Liquibase), or worked with pg_dump internals.

Open-source maintainer experience: API contracts, semver, documentation, and community triage.

Familiarity with Postgres extensions such as pg_cron, pgmq, pg_partman, vault, pg_net, pgvector, or PostGIS.

Contributions to Postgres itself, or authorship of a Postgres extension.

Experience with Supabase or another managed Postgres platform.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#6947. Well, It Sure Seems Like Mitigating Climate Change Is No Longer a Concern for Democrats

Conservative Republicans and their oligarchical allies are getting everything they want out of a bipartisan bill that could walk back important environmental laws.

Last Thursday, and on a bipartisan basis, the Senate approved the so-called Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA), an alleged effort to “reform” the federal permitting process for things like energy projects, hard-rock mining, and, yes, data centers. At the very least, the bill lived up to one part of its clumsy title; its passage through the Senate truly was bipartisan. It passed by a 62–33 vote, and liberal Democratic senator Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island was one of its strongest advocates.

It’s also a very big apple with more than a few razor blades concealed inside. Environmentalists have been giving BAAJA the side-eye for a number of reasons. Out in Nebraska, Democratic state chair Jane Fleming Kleeb suspects the bill is connected to a sudden revival of interest in restarting our old friend, the Keystone XL pipeline, the continent-spanning death funnel meant to bring the world’s dirtiest fossil fuel from Alberta ultimately to refineries around the world.

My problem with this whole thing is that this “kind of civil war” is taking place exclusively on the Left. Conservative Republicans, and their oligarchical allies, are forcing the action before the new Democratic majorities take over in January. They know exactly what they want out of the bill and what they don’t want in it. The president already has said there won’t be any wind farms under the provisions of this bill on his watch. (The birds! The whales!) The bill now is with the House of Representatives. May I suggest a discreet dragging of the feet? It is the holiday season, after all.

#6940. What’s a Vintage Car Rally Really Like? Inside the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run.

How I got mixed up in a race with a bunch of priceless historic automobiles and views of Italy’s spectacular Alpine scenery—and won.

In vintage rally driving, there are generally two types of competitor. Both share a passion for keeping priceless and nearly century-old vehicles in use on the open road rather than displaying them under glass. But some see the drive itself as challenge enough, while stalwarts adhere to what’s called the road book. This is a hundred or so pages of complex navigational directions that the copilot uses to navigate instead of a map, maintaining a constant flow of instructions to the driver. It is this book and how closely you follow it that decides the eventual winner.

Gianandrea Redaelli, a car nut whose family has long roots in the Italian printing business, is firmly in the “road book” camp. In early September, I climbed into the copilot seat of his 1939 Aston Martin C-Type Speed Model Roadster Bertelli—just one of two ever built, with an experimental aerodynamic body that earned it the nickname “the Flying Pea”—and stayed there as he drove six hundred kilometers of “left at the traffic lights, right at the cow” for the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run. The rally started in Venice and ended, after a scenic but challenging detour high into the Dolomite mountains, at Monza, the home of Italian Formula 1, just in time for practice day of the Grand Prix.

Venice might not seem the most logical starting line for a car rally given the proverbial wetness of the streets. But the city was chosen this year because it was also the end point of the last LV rally of its kind: the La Serenissima Run from Monte Carlo to Venice in 2012. Gianandrea won that one as copilot, with his father, Giuseppe, at the wheel. The pair also won the one before it—the Boheme across Hungary, the Czech Republic, and Austria—in 2006. This time, however, the pressure was on me too.

I have been a copilot before, and I can tell you it’s no picnic. Rally racing in vintage cars is less about speed and more about timing. It’s an exercise that requires pinpoint accuracy at the time-stamp points in the overall course and, most critically, in the regularities. These are fiendishly short time-over-distance sections in which crossing the line just a few hundredths of a second late—or early—earns strict penalties. Sensibly, the day before the race began, Gianandrea took me out in the Aston in the Veneto countryside to practice, especially those critical regularities.

I could have opted for an easy life. The rally allowed competing cars, dating from 1931 to 1969, to use the road book or follow the course using a map in a dedicated phone app. But where’s the fun in that?

“If you join a competition, you follow the regulations and strive to do your best,” Gianandrea says. “As an Italian, if you paint numbers on the door of my car, I will want to win.”

Every car in a rally like this comes with its own idiosyncrasies. On flat ground, someone driving a souped-up 2026 sports car would find these tests difficult to nail. In the reality of race conditions, the regularities often involve steep climbs or descents and unexpected corners, alling for an expert hand at the wheel—and foot on the gas pedal. The tendency of some car doors to swing open at hard bends (ours has the “suicide” doors with hinges on the back instead of the front, beloved by early carmakers) only adds to the excitement. The purpose of driver and copilot is to battle and overcome all the mechanical and computational challenges—and get our ride to the finish line bang on time.

Louis Vuitton has been involved in these glamorous and exacting vintage rallies since 1993. The Dolomites Run represents an enthusiastic return to the genre after a fourteen-year gap and, perhaps tellingly, the company’s global sponsorship (since 2025) of Formula 1. Unconfirmed rumors circulated in the field that the choice of ending the rally at a Grand Prix could well be repeated—and soon—in other countries in the future.

It’s easy, after all, to see why a fusion of style and cars with six- and seven-figure price tags might appeal to Louis Vuitton and competitors alike. Not surprisingly, at every coffee and lunch stop, the LV effect sprinkled the race with an added sense of luxury. In between, our snaking caravan carried the glamour of a century of motoring along with it. At every hairpin you got a glimpse of a gull-wing Mercedes, a 1931 Bugatti two-seater (my personal fave), and a shimmering behemoth of a Bentley in quick succession. These cars would turn heads anywhere. But in Italy, arguably the home of the sports car, they brought smiles, applause, and gasps in even the tiniest village that passed by in our slipstream. I swear I even saw a nun with a glint in her eye at one set of traffic lights.

Navigating, I was quickly learning, is not so much about providing a constant babble of information as it is knowing how to give clear and simple instructions—and only when strictly needed. As the finish neared in Monza at the end of two long days on the road, I was able to give Gianandrea the most helpful instruction of all: “Nothing for 15 kilometers.”

“That,” he very kindly replied, “is the mark of a pro.”

Even after the engines cooled, we were still clueless about how well we’d done overall. That changed at the final event, a dinner inside the Sforzesco Castle in Milan. Gianandrea stepped up, beaming, as winner of the race, and for the first time as driver. I stood somewhat sheepishly behind him as copilot as he received the trophy from Pietro Beccari, CEO of both Louis Vuitton and Louis Vuitton Fashion Group. It was an impressive swirl of Murano glass created by Venini, housed in its own equally impressive traveling case, made (naturellement) at Louis Vuitton’s atelier in Asnières.

Gianandrea has promised me visiting rights.

#6936. Does Anyone Care How Yellowjackets Ends Anymore?

The survival drama is finally back for its last season. But finding out the answers to the show’s mysteries isn’t all that exciting anymore.

Believe it or not, Yellowjackets is based on real events. In 1972, a high school rugby team, along with their families, boarded Uruguayan Air Force Flight 571 and crashed in the Andes. Only sixteen of the forty-five passengers survived when they were rescued seventy-two days later, and they all harbored a dark secret: To survive, they ate the flesh of the people who had died.

Yellowjackets, in a way, explores what might have happened had the group never revealed just how they managed to survive in the wilderness. Much like Uruguayan Air Force Flight 571, the Showtime series follows a high school women’s soccer team that survives a plane crash by killing and eating each other. Cocreators Ashley Lyle and Bart Nickerson blended in potential supernatural elements such as a dark presence that possibly forces them to kill, classic Lord of the Flies homages, and a killer soundtrack. Yellowjackets also cut ahead to each survivor’s life as an adult, featuring the acting talents of Melanie Lynskey, Christina Ricci, and Juliette Lewis. For a while, it was engrossing TV.

The series explored survival in all its forms, from the lasting effects of grief to the need for women to escape their mundane, suburban lives for a little danger. It was critically acclaimed, nominated for awards, and topped many “Best of the Year” lists. But then, five years and two more chaotic seasons went by that really seemed to test its audience’s … well, appetites.

Now that the fourth and final season is upon us—season 4 premiered on Friday, November 20—the answers to Yellowjackets’ burning questions will finally be revealed. Namely: Is something forcing them to kill each other, or can everything be explained by science? Will the public find out that they resorted to ritualistic murder and cannibalism? And, most importantly, is there anyone left who’s still interested in finding out?

To be fair, there’s already something of an answer to that last question. Despite critical fatigue for the series, over two million viewers tuned in to the season 3 premiere last year, per Paramount. That number then increased a bit to 3 million by the season 3 finale. Sure, those numbers pale in comparison to The Pitt season 2’s 15.4 million average weekly viewers or even the Landman season 2 premiere’s 9.2 million viewers. But there is a fan base willing to see this series to the end, whatever happens.

What is that ending? Where the series currently stands, our girls are finally returning home after being rescued. They’ll likely adjust to normal life again by committing another hunt back in their hometown. And in the present, the promise of “last one standing” in the pre-roll recap likely points to a final bloodbath. Is that even what fans want? Until that finale, how everything will resolve, and what it all really means, remains a mystery.

For the Uruguayans, they faced a moral and religious crisis when they returned. When news broke that the rugby team ate the flesh of their dead passengers, one of the survivors defended their actions by comparing it to that of Jesus Christ and the Eucharist. According to the survivor, the group made a pact to sacrifice their bodies for each other if they perished so that they could eat their flesh to survive. The media circus completely flipped, and they were heroes once again. Pope Paul VI even wrote them a letter to sanctify their actions. It’s a fascinating end to their story—but one that the Yellowjackets cocreators likely found made for an entirely different sort of TV show.

Outside of the pagan imagery and supernatural threats that the Yellowjackets cast face, religion has yet to enter the picture. The cannibalism mostly seemed inevitable until it finally happened halfway through season 2, and besides that, the women have done much worse since returning home. After three seasons of one cold-blooded murder after another, the body count just to keep their secret hidden is enough for all of them to face the electric chair. And even if there is a malevolent force controlling them, it may take a metaphorical release of pain and grief on par with Hereditary or Weapons to fully satisfy viewers in the series finale.

“To some extent, I do feel as though if we were to just 100 percent jump both feet on one side of [the supernatural/reality] line, it would disperse some of what makes this show interesting,” cocreator Ashley Lyle told CBR ahead of season 4.

“I think that we are fully aware that we are either going to delight or anger both sides of that equation in some way, shape, or form,” they added. “But I don’t know that that could have been avoided.”

At this point, some version of watching the women all kill each other, or even watching them all defeat the evil they believe is haunting them, just doesn’t feel like enough. The show’s biggest problem might have just been that it ran too long. A simpler ending to a shorter story might have left viewers delighted. But we shall see! If Yellowjackets can land the plane (sorry) in the end, I’m willing to eat (apologies again) my own words on this one.

#6920. Most Black Friday Sales Are Fake. These Ones Aren’t.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Internet is a deep, dark place this week, but we found where the deals are worth it.

How do you even define a good deal anymore? The whole point of Black Friday was for retailers to make us wake up at the crack of dawn the day after Thanksgiving, wait in line, and then fight someone over a TV that was 50-percent off at Sears or Best Buy. How did they do that? They would sell a few products (door busters) at a loss just to physically bring people into the store. Those deals were good, and everything else, which you would buy because you were already there, was where the money was made.

That is now a thing of the past. Lines and physical altercations aside, in the past 20 years there has been a real swing in the favor of big retailers. First, we created Cyber Monday (though most people do their Black Friday shopping online already). Then the deals started a week earlier. Now, they last all month. And instead of having door busters to get you in the door, retailers just have very aggressive online marketing programs. In fact, it turns out that most Black Friday deals aren’t even deals at all. In a recent study, it was found that one in three Amazon Black Friday deals provided no true savings at all—prices were marked up earlier in the year, only to be brought back down to a standard retail value. The big retailers like Amazon and Walmart are the most to blame for these tricky strategies, but you should still be judicious when looking at any so-called Black Friday sales.

The good news? Black Friday is still filled with great deals—you just have to know where to look and what to expect. A lot of big wins revolve around private sales at more niche, direct-to-consumer stores. For example, Filson, one of our favorite outerwear brands, is having a 25-percent-off sale for Black Friday. That’s not the 75-percent-off you might have wanted, and there are other times that Filson goes 25-percent off. But the best strategy these days is to think about holiday shopping: Just buy the things you already would, but do it while they’re on sale now.

You should be especially skeptical of tech products. The increase in all tech prices has made it easy for those brands to push a 10-percent-off sale when products where 10-percent-off the current price. This is the sort of stuff we’ve been watching as the sales roll around. Based on this and years of covering sales and knowing what’s good and what’s not, we determined these are the best deals of the weekend. These are the deals our editors think are actually worth shopping this Black Friday.

Mr. Porter has an absolute goldmine of a sale. Like sure, you can grab this Brunello Cucinelli Jacket for $6,500 at 50-percent off. But it’s also filled with basics from brands like On that rarely go on sale.

These Nike socks are more fun than the pairs they sell on amazon. And they’re $6. Also better than Amazon.

$644 for a cashmere-blend overcoat that’s normally $1,298 is a real-life steal.

A few days ago, the Samsung Frame Pro was 50-percent off. That deal was real, and it sold out. Today the Hisense Art TV (which we’re fans of) is 50-percent off. These deals vary daily, but today, this is the one we’d buy.

Dyson deals are confusing as fuck. You need to be knowledgeable about the model you’re buying into, and here that number matters. You will be able to find models like the V8 and the V10 for super cheap this week—but that’s because those models are old and outdated. Right now, the best and newest Dyson cordless vacuum is the V16—with all the bells and whistles it retails at $1,100, but it’s $200 off right now.

But as someone who has used every Dyson, I know the V12 is a damn good model. And while it isn’t the newest, it still has lots of the same features as newer models and will last you years. So the fact that it’s on sale for $400 this weekend makes it a truly good deal. Also, I can’t find this deal on Amazon. For the best deals from Dyson, I would always check its actual site.

As previously mentioned, we love that many of Filson’s jackets are 25-percent off this week. This is a good example of when a brand just wants to participate in a sale, so they’re willing to mark down popular items. This jacket is so good our commerce editor Luke Guillory endorsed it last week, calling it the best $250 jacket. Guess what? Now it’s only $187. We know for a fact that this deal won’t last past Black Friday.

It’s expensive, yes, but Begg x Co. is one of the best cashmere brands anywhere in the world. $500 off a new cashmere sweater is something that doesn’t happen until the end of the season. But if you buy it now, you still have six good months of wear to get in.

Water filtration has become very expensive, especially when it comes to reverse osmosis and a countertop version that looks sleek. We have been recommending this one for over a year at $500, so the fact that it’s $150-off for once makes it a pretty good deal.

Furniture on sale is a whole category we can’t ignore. Furniture markups are a real thing, and a lot of it has to do with quality and materials. Marble is overpriced for sure, but good quality marble is worth it. We love Soho Home’s marble, and this dining table has been one of its most popular pieces for a while. Getting it for almost $3k-off when you’re not a member is a big deal.

If you’re on the home redo kick, then a brand like Le Maé, which is offering some its best wood pieces at 50-percent off, is a great place to start. Made with high quality materials and oak, this is one of those furniture pieces you will own forever.

If you’re a camo guy or do any non-deal hunting, then you know that Tom Beckbe jackets never go on sale—but this is your lucky week.

#6918. AI Native Right Hand of the CEO - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

You dream of a new challenge, of being able to make a company scale and feel that you have a real impact.

If you dream of a life that is more like an adventure than a stable life, then becoming the AI-native right hand of our CEO is for you.

This isn’t another chief of staff or right hand job.

Most companies call themselves AI-first because they gave everyone a ChatGPT licence. We think that’s level 2, and there are 10.

At Paradox our ambition is $10M in revenue with 10 people, each of them running a dozen agents. Here, AI doesn’t assist the work. It runs the loops, in every department. Your job is to make that true for the whole company, side by side with the CEO.

Deep inside you are looking for a new adventure with…

🗽Freedom: working remotely or hybrid, and being trusted to deliver

🔥Ambition: building one of the first truly AI-native companies, with teammates who push AI to its limits every week

😍Impact: changing the trajectory of a whole company, not one line of a roadmap, with a product that transforms people’s lives at scale

Then, you have found the next adventure that will fulfill you.

In a company of fewer than 10 people, the right hand used to be the person who absorbed everything the CEO couldn’t do. Here, the right hand builds the machine that does it.

Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, offers, decisions, procedures, member stories and past experiments, in one shared company brain that every person and every agent reads before acting.

Turn every decision into execution. A meeting, a voice note or a call with the CEO becomes briefs, tickets, follow-ups and deadlines, without anyone retyping it.

Act across every tool. Not a chatbot in a tab: agents read and write in our whole stack, from code to CRM, billing, analytics, ads, email, calendar and documents.

Ship. Produce the analysis, the contract draft, the page, the offer or the process, and put it live.

Measure with real KPIs. Revenue, cash, margin, retention and the transformation of our members, not activity.

Learn and loop on its own. Keep what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and start the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

People set the ambition, the taste and the standards. The machine runs the loop, and gets better every week.

This is already real. Our company brain holds our decisions and procedures. Our growth hub tracks every hypothesis and experiment with its result. Agents cut ads from long-form content, prepare webinars and audit our tracking after every live. And our product is AI-native too: Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day, by text and by voice.

We believe self-help is broken. Most people use learning platforms the same way: generic theory, a quick high, no real change.

They deserve more than content and more than motivation.

They deserve a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges and grows with them.

Human Intelligence (coaches, peers and mentors)

Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

into one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS, the Operating System for Human Greatness, augmented with AI.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world, and for something that will outlive any individual, its founder included.

Here’s the bet: A small team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

Reporting directly to the founder and CEO, you will help him run and grow the company.

Your scope is as wide as a CEO/COO’s: strategy, operations, finance, legal, people, product marketing and launches.

The difference is how you do it. In a classic company, the right hand coordinates people and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls. With fewer than 10 people, there is no department to hand things to. You build it.

❌The old way: a chief of staff takes notes in meetings, chases people by email and builds the weekly report by hand.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every CEO decision into execution. Meetings, voice notes and calls become briefs, tickets and follow-ups. Every Monday, the CEO gets one brief: what moved, what’s stuck, and what needs his call.

You decide what the CEO must see and what he never needs to see again. His time goes to vision, product and members, not to coordination.

Depending on your zone of genius, these are examples of strategic projects you could own:

Growth: run experiments with agents that generate, ship and measure them

Partnerships & sponsorships: find and close partners aligned with our brand, for our YouTube channel and events

International: build the machine that turns our French content into English, and open new countries

Launches: take a new offer or product from idea to live

People: find and assess the rare people who join a team of fewer than 10

❌The old way: each new initiative needs a team, a roadmap and three months.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: one person with the right agents launches an offer, a channel or a country without disrupting the core. That’s what our Pirate Squad means now.

❌The old way: an ops manager processes invoices, chases contracts, onboards people and answers the same support questions every week.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: finance, legal, HR, admin and customer support each get their agents, their context, their KPIs and their loop. You write the skills, plug them into our tools and write the evals that define "good": a reconciliation that matches to the cent, a contract that respects our playbook, an answer a member is happy with.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. An agent moves up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month, you build the agent that reconciles payments, refunds and failed charges every night. In month one it only reports. By month three it fixes the simple cases alone, and you only see the exceptions.

❌The old way: someone builds dashboards in a BI tool, exports them to a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody fully trusts the numbers.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: agents watch revenue, cash, acquisition, retention, churn and competitors continuously, find where our sources disagree, and explain what changed and why.

Strategy: screen markets and competition, build growth models, explore business models, partnerships and acquisitions. Then challenge, formalize and ensure good execution of the strategy.

At Paradox things move fast, and our CEO is always reading business books and meeting great entrepreneurs. You’ll turn the best strategies and tactics into systems and agents the whole company uses.

❌The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: every decision, result and lesson goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before acting. The company never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: the share of work done without a human, the time from decision to execution, the agents that moved up an autonomy level this month, and revenue per person. You push those numbers up.

You help the team become AI-native too: you spot the work still done by hand, and you build the agent or show a teammate how to build it.

And always: our members trust us with their lives. No fake claims, no fake scarcity, data handled with care. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. People do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. The company’s output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

Top tier Business or Engineering School (HEC, ESSEC, INSEAD, EM Lyon, EDHEC, ESCP, Stanford, …) or other high-level academic training (Les Mines, X, Sciences Po Paris, etc.)

A wide-scope role behind you: founder, chief of staff, founder’s associate, strategy, consulting or operations in a fast-growing startup

You’ve built with AI and agents yourself: Claude Code, agent SDKs, MCP, automations, APIs, evals. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

Driven with strong work ethic: this position requires a strong commitment and perseverance

Entrepreneurship spirit: able to own a project from scratch, with few resources and little guidance, doing a lot yourself

Builder, not coordinator: you’d rather build the agent than chase a person twice

Taste: you know the difference between an output that works and one that is great, and you hold agents to the second

Growth over ego: you are at ease with regular feedback, obsessed with learning and growing, able to put your ego aside

Analytical and system thinker: you quickly identify and formulate problems, and solve complex ones with data

Excels in communication: both written and verbal

French: native or bilingual. English: fluent, you can hold a detailed conversation with English-speaking colleagues. Our chosen language for written communication is English.

We are inspired by a culture of performance and ambition. Everything is fast-paced and there is a lot of change. You won’t always be told what to do or how to do it.

We tell each other things, even if they are uncomfortable. We value growth over ego. At Paradox, expressing disagreement to a manager and knowing how to debate is valued! We encourage critical and strategic thinking.

❌ If you use AI for first drafts but your way of working looks the same as two years ago: wrong role.

❌ If you want to manage a lot of people: we aim for $10M in revenue with 10 people, each managing a dozen agents.

❌ If you need clarity before chaos: wrong place.

If all this makes sense with you, then we’ll love working with you (and you with us, you’ll be pampered!)

Build one of the first truly AI-native companies from inside the CEO’s office, and write the playbook others will copy. You’ll compete with San Francisco companies.

🏅Being surrounded by A-players who strive to perform, whilst being in a good mood

🏋️‍♀️No more bosses, but ongoing conversations to support your growth

⚡️Learning to build with AI faster than almost anywhere else

🦄Playing a crucial role in the development of a company whose content and products are used by millions of people

#6915. Software Engineer - Interfaces Backend at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer to join Supabase’s Interfaces group and help run the backend services behind our developer tools, starting with postgres-meta: the open-source service that runs SQL against customers’ Postgres databases on behalf of our dashboard, our MCP server, AI agents, and other platform tools. You’ll work closely with the engineers who build those tools, and with our Platform and SRE teams.

AI agents are the fastest-growing source of postgres-meta’s traffic, and it sits on the path between developers and their data, so when it struggles, people can’t reach their databases from the dashboard or their agents. The team’s first priority is making it reliable, observable, and fair between tenants at agent scale, and helping redesign it for that scale without breaking the tools that depend on it. Depending on your experience, you’ll own major parts of that work or lead it. Alongside it, the team builds the backend services that give every branch of a project the configuration and secrets it needs.

We want to be upfront: this role carries real operational load. Our on-call process and rotation are new and need a lot of maturing, and you’ll help build them into something sustainable from your first weeks. You’ll be on call for services on the critical path, and incidents come before planned work. Platform teams run the infrastructure underneath; you’ll own the services themselves, from their code to how they behave in production. It’s ideal for someone who enjoys that kind of ownership, thrives in async, fast-paced environments, and stays calm when the root cause turns out to be in someone else’s system.

Help mature the team’s on-call from your first weeks: grow the rotation to cover every service the team runs, write runbooks, and agree when to declare an incident. Then build it, with our embedded SRE, into a sustainable follow-the-sun rotation as the team grows.

Set SLOs with the teams that call the service, and build the observability to hold them: metrics per tenant and per caller, alerts that catch failures wherever they start, and logs you can search across regions.

Make deploys routine with canary and staged rollouts, so dependency and security upgrades stop being risky.

Redesign how the service works with Postgres at scale: reusing connections, limiting or streaming large results, and isolating and rate-limiting tenants, so no single caller, human or agent, can degrade it for everyone else.

Help decide the service’s long-term architecture with our Platform teams, then evolve it without breaking the callers that depend on it.

Write down and agree the service’s contract with its callers, including the dashboard, MCP and AI, and platform workers: how transactions, multi-statement queries, timeouts, and errors behave.

Work through incidents with the networking, Kubernetes, pooler, and control-plane teams when a problem on the path to customers’ databases starts in their systems.

Build backend services for code-first workflows, starting with delivering each branch’s configuration and secrets to the services that need them.

Make small changes directly in the CLI, dashboard, or docs when a capability needs them, with the owning team reviewing.

Review code and designs, mentor other engineers, and grow into leading the team’s technical direction.

Have 5+ years of experience as a software engineer, most of it building and running backend services in production.

Have run a high-traffic, multi-tenant service in production: been on call for it, worked incidents through to resolution, and followed through on postmortems.

Write strong TypeScript, and work confidently in a large Node.js backend codebase.

Understand Postgres from the client side: connection management and pooling, transactions, statement timeouts and cancellation, and large result sets.

Have built observability for a service you owned (metrics, tracing, logs, and alerts tied to SLOs), and used it to debug problems that span proxies, load balancers, or Kubernetes.

Have kept a service healthy under load with patterns such as rate limiting, timeouts, memory limits, and graceful degradation.

Have shipped changes to a live service safely, with canaries, staged rollouts, feature flags, or fast rollback.

Work calmly and constructively with other teams during incidents, including when the root cause is in a system you don’t own.

Communicate clearly in writing, from design docs to postmortems, thrive in async, globally distributed teams, and make progress when the path isn’t fully defined.

Nice to have, roughly in order of how much they’d help:

Have rebuilt or migrated a live, high-traffic service without breaking its callers, using techniques such as shadow traffic, contract tests, or incremental replacement.

Have led the design of reliability work, such as tenant isolation, load shedding, or a staged-deploy pipeline, or set technical direction that other engineers built on.

Have built or operated a database proxy or connection pooler (such as PgBouncer, Supavisor, or pgcat), or an API gateway such as Envoy.

Have secured a service that runs SQL on its callers’ behalf: handling credentials safely, and limiting how much damage any one caller can do.

Production experience in a second backend language, such as Elixir or Go.

Experience with a secrets manager or key management service, such as HashiCorp Vault or AWS KMS.

Familiarity with Supabase, or with how AI agents use databases through tools like MCP.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#6914. This New Jaeger-LeCoultre Watch Looks Perfect for Robert Pattinson’s James Bond

The actor’s favorite watchmaker has unveiled a pink-gold Reverso with impeccable timing.

Just a week after Robert Pattinson was announced as the next James Bond, Jaeger-LeCoultre, the Swiss watchmaker for which he's an ambassador, has unveiled a new Reverso that looks rather well suited to 007.

It's certainly a good match for Pattinson's personal tastes. “I definitely like really thin watches,” he said in a recent interview. “That's the main thing. In general, I would probably prefer a leather strap.”

The new Reverso Classic Monoface Small Seconds checks both boxes. Measuring just 7.56mm thick, it has an 18-carat pink-gold case, a silver-colored dial with a texture inspired by stacked coins, and a black alligator strap. Its smaller proportions–40.1mm by 24.4mm—recall the original Reverso of 1931, whose ingenious swiveling case was designed to protect the watch during polo matches. Inside is a manually wound movement with a 42-hour power reserve.

At $18,400, it's a somewhat more elegant alternative to the more beefy dive watches traditionally associated with Bond. And with its art deco styling and slim profile, it would slip neatly beneath a dinner-jacket cuff with an ease the next Madeleine Swann would surely appreciate.

Of course, Omega has been Bond's official watch partner since 1995, and there's no suggestion that's changing. But as a watch for the man playing Bond, this new Reverso feels intriguingly on point.

#6901. Bono and Anton Corbijn Open Up About the Creative Partnership That Changed Rock ’n’ Roll History

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It is the most extensive collaboration ever between a band and a visual artist. Here, the frontman and photographer take Esquire behind the scenes of the album covers, music videos, and photos shoots that have defined their careers.

“Bono is usually a man with a lot of ideas,” says photographer/director Anton Corbijn, “so initially we have a conversation and then maybe I make it more theatrical or whatever.”

That overly casual-sounding process has led to the most extensive collaboration ever between a band and a visual artist. Since their first encounter in 1982, Corbijn has helped establish the evolving public image of U2 across album covers, music videos, and photo shoots for almost 45 years, including the landmark cover of The Joshua Tree, the all-out aesthetic reboot for Achtung Baby, and music videos including “Pride (In the Name of Love)” and “One.” (The partnership is extensively documented in Corbijn’s 2005 book U2&i.) Most recently, they reconnected for a connected pair of surreal and evocative videos—“The Greatest Show on Earth” and “Can’t Stop Us Now”—from U2’s new album Carnaval de Luz, the band’s first full-length release in nine years.

Bono recalls first noticing Corbijn’s work when he was still a teenager, especially a photo of Joy Division that ran on the cover of NME in 1980 following the suicide of lead singer Ian Curtis. “Seeing Anton make these images of these people, they were actual human beings,” he says. “We think from their music that they’re certainly not, but then these images prove that they’re people who lived just across the way. It’s like making movies.”

Indeed, Corbijn has gone on to direct films, including the acclaimed 2007 Ian Curtis biopic Control, as well as doing videos and album covers for Nirvana, Tom Waits, Metallica, the Rolling Stones, and R.E.M. and decades of ongoing work with Depeche Mode.

“It’s difficult for a photographer to shoot bands,” he says, “because bands have an identity, and you need to somehow get that right. It’s very important that the pictures I do with Depeche Mode don’t look like the pictures I do with U2.”

Recently, Bono (in New York City) and Corbijn (in Amsterdam) met up on Zoom to discuss their unprecedented alliance, playfully referring to each other as “A” and “B” and recalling shared moments and mutual inspiration. (They also revealed that Corbijn made a film about the American leg of the 2017 Joshua Tree 30th-anniversary tour, which they are planning to bring to local screens during next year’s Carnaval de Luz: The Greatest Show on Earth dates.)

“There are, of course, many occasions where a great artist springs up on their own—a painter, a songwriter, a photographer, a filmmaker, novelist,” says Bono. “But often there’s some kind of community. U2’s music certainly comes out of community, and Anton is a huge part of our community.”

This conversation was edited for length and clarity.

Bono: The great stories in songwriting are often unexplained, not resolved. And Anton, your gift to U2 was that you want the emotions to cohere rather than to explain the music. If everything adds up, it adds up to nothing. It’s the thing that you can’t explain that keeps you going back to the work—which sounds like that might be an excuse for laziness, for half-finished lyrics, but I felt over our time together that you finished some of the lyrics for me with your images.

The comedy and the tragedy of “The Greatest Show on Earth” is a real example of that. I hope it’s not too close to the bone with the singer playing the leader of a religious cult and the women doing all the work. But look, anyone who can get the members of U2 to put on beards and look like ZZ Top is some sort of shaman. To do one of your film clips is to risk being very silly—and whilst I don’t have that fear in general, as is obvious from the pratfalls I’ve made in my life and artistic life, the other three members of the band do have a certain amount of self-respect, and so only their faith in you as an artist and trust in you as a friend would have them participate at the level they did.

Anton Corbijn: I don’t want to make anybody look silly, but weird is good, and strange, and like you said. I have a very intuitive approach, and we collaborate. Famously, what became the cover of The Joshua Tree was me looking at the desert for a photograph for an album U2 were calling Desert Songs.

Bono: Yeah, yeah, that’s right. Another [potential] title was The Two Americas. But wasn’t it on that shoot we found the title? Via you, and especially the way you pronounced “Yah-shua Tree.” I hope you still find it amusing that we find your Dutch accent amusing.

But as often happens in art, the reason becomes clear years later. That’s the strangest thing—it didn’t start with the Joshua tree; it’s at the end you discovered it. On Carnaval de Luz, we knew from the beginning what we were thinking about, but we discovered it at the very end with the image maker, with the icon maker.

Corbijn: I remember we had a three-day shoot, and we were driving from Reno to Palm Springs, and after the first day, we walked together to the hotel in the desert. And I told you about this tree that I liked so much. I had this landscape camera where the lens moved, so you could get very long shots. I thought it was really nice that you have a tree on the front, and the image goes on to the back, and the band is on the back. So I was talking about that, and I mentioned the name of that tree. And then, the next morning, you came down with the Bible in your hand and said, “It’s got to be The Joshua Tree!”

Bono: We had no idea that Joshua is the same name as Jesus, Yeshua, and what post-punk band would put an album out called The Jesus Tree? But we forget, because the world is going through some parched times presently, that even though the ’80s seemed to be great for a lot of people—you had the Material Girl and dance music, and New York was flying—there was something up in the ’80s. Springsteen would say there was a darkness on the edge of town. And I think for us to dress as pilgrims, as Irish people coming to America, was absurd but actually very effective.

We were trying to figure out, How do you not look stupid on a magazine cover? How are you to be? I mean, we were in our early 20s, and we were not elegantly wasted, as was kind of the mood of the time—if you’re in a rock ’n’ roll band, you should have the good manners to at least look like you’re about to die any minute. You don’t want to look earnest. And we needed to find our own way of, How do we be ourselves? What masks can we choose to reveal ourselves? What is the iconography of whatever the project is? That naturally led on to Zoo TV and all the various shoots we did for Achtung Baby and Zooropa and all that.

My favorite session of ours, Anton, is when we shot for Pop in Miami. They’re some of my favorite photographs of the band because you seem to photograph what the work is rather than what the people are. So for Pop, you went for bright colors and mischief and charm. Anton, do you remember those sessions in Miami?

Corbijn: Yeah, vividly actually, because you were still recording at the time, in the basement in a hotel. We were young and playful, and so many different options with clothing as well. And we tried funny-looking shots and playful shots, serious stuff as well, but I think after The Joshua Tree, I was attempting to bring more life into the pictures with color use. I also remember you saying that at the time that you liked the colorful stuff I did with Depeche. It was not new for me, but we did cross-processing so the color became very vivid, and that’s why Achtung Baby looks so colorful.

Bono: We always live by this sort of modus that the songs will tell us what to do. The songs will tell us how to look, how to behave, what the live show is about, how to dress onstage and sometimes even offstage—and because your thing was, “I’m shooting the song; I’m shooting the material, not you,” I think that really worked for us.

One thing I’d like to ask you, Anton: Would you be comfortable talking about the religiosity of your work? It’s a recurring theme for you, and can you explain a little bit of that?

Corbijn: Not really, actually. I don’t go to church anymore. My father was a pastor, and my grandfather, and all my uncles, and so it sort of made me walk away from it. But I think because I grew up with it, it’s just in my visual language. We grew up next to a cemetery, and maybe that had something to do with it, but the visual iconography, the Christian iconography, is in our society so much that it’s hard to not use it. Sometimes it’s just there—like the first pictures I did with Depeche Mode in ’81, when they were a teeny-tiny pop band, was in this studio in London, and there was this cross with Jesus on it in the garden. So in the photograph, they were like teenybop guys, but still Christ is hanging in the picture.

Bono: Religion has often been such a negative force in the world. It’s very understandable that we would try to avoid the obvious imagery associated with it, but in my life it’s turned into a very positive thing. It’s sort of buoyed me up. But you have it in all your work, despite your attempt to get away from it, I suppose.

Corbijn: I was just thinking, in the “One” video, there’s the Father, Son, and the Holy Ghost, and the band, and the woman. It was all in there. I think it’s very nice that I knew your father and you met my father.

Bono: Yes, that’s right. Two powerful men in our lives. But again, your ability to talk people into doing things—you asked my father to stand on a seesaw, and he went along with it, he was fascinated by it. And then you got the four fathers of U2, the fathers and sons.

Corbijn: That was one of my favorite pictures of U2. There’s a lot of depth in it.

Bono: What other favorites do you have, Anton?

Corbijn: Well, I made a book of 500 pages on U2, full of pictures I like. I like some of the photographs we did around Rattle and Hum. That was not a good time for me, but I think there’s some good pictures there. Also on the rooftop in L.A.—I don’t mean the playing one, but the one when we looked at the rooftop, when we discovered it.

Bono: I found this hotel, a real hotel called the Rosslyn Million Dollar Hotel. I was reading Bukowski at the time, listening to Tom Waits, and I walked into this hotel. I couldn’t believe it existed. Eventually I managed to convince Wim Wenders to make a film there. And we so loved the sign, the Million Dollar Hotel, that when we played on that rooftop, we rebuilt the sign.

Corbijn: Yeah but on a much smaller rooftop. I’m in the video, actually. I’m trying to get through the crowd, pointing to the policeman that I have a pass, and he goes, “You go that way!”

Bono: That poor man, that policeman who pulled the plug. By all accounts, his children tortured him, and he was the most unpopular cop in Los Angeles. He was just doing his job.

Let me see if I one more question for my hero and friend...

AI is increasingly part of everyday leadership work, from drafting emails and reports to organizing ideas. Used thoughtfully, it can reduce administrative burden. But when AI enters more personal communication—feedback, recognition, recommendations, and appraisals—the question is no longer simple.

#6897. It’s Time to Take Full Stock of Bond 26

Robert Pattinson is the new 007, with Denis Villeneuve directing and Steven Knight writing. From the villain to a possible release date, here’s what we know about Bond’s next chapter.

The search for the new James Bond was one of Hollywood’s most closely scrutinized casting processes. Producer Amy Pascal said the team was being “really, really methodical” in its search for Craig’s successor, and the shortlist of hopefuls was as long as Bond’s list of ex-flings, with about the same survival rate.

Josh O’Connor was an early favorite until he stepped back in October, saying he planned a career break in 2027; the Daily Mail later reported that he had turned the role down. Elsewhere, the betting and the gossip circled Slow Horses heartthrob Jack Lowden, Jacob Elordi, James Norton, Jack O’Connell, and Masters of the Air’s Callum Turner.

But after all that Bond-esque bluffing and counterbluffing from Amazon MGM Studios, Robert Pattinson was unveiled as the new 007 in mid-November. Everybody (mostly) breathed a sigh of relief—and turned their attention to the rest of the upcoming reboot. So, what do we know so far?

The second crucial element of Le Nouveau Bond is that French-Canadian director Denis Villeneuve will direct. He, too, has pedigree, not least his epic three-part Dune series, the first two films of which have already grossed more than $1 billion worldwide, with Part Three due out in December. “If there’s one thing we can be sure of with Denis, it’s that he will do something interesting with this,” a source within the film industry told me. “He is one of those directors who can turn even a bad script into a good film.”

Speaking of which, the other major name already signed up to the reimagining is Peaky Blinders creator Steven Knight. He will write the story.

Knight seems a natural fit for a darker, more character-driven Bond. His breakout was the screenplay for Dirty Pretty Things, which earned him an Oscar nomination. He went on to write David Cronenberg’s brutal Eastern Promises and the one-man thriller Locke.

But it was Peaky Blinders that made his name: a stylized, violent, brooding saga of a damaged man at the rotten heart of a criminal empire, which became a global hit for the BBC and Netflix. Knight, in other words, has previous when it comes to writing moody, morally murky men.

No need to say much—we know what we’re dealing with here. A man who makes good choices. A star.

At forty, Pattinson is a little older than Craig was when he got the job in 2005, aged thirty-seven. But the career behind him is hardly that of a conventional leading man. After the teen-idol years of Twilight, he spent a decade deliberately steering away from the obvious: Cronenberg’s Cosmopolis, the Safdie brothers’ Good Time, Robert Eggers’s The Lighthouse, and Claire Denis’s High Life, before returning to the blockbuster fold with Christopher Nolan’s Tenet, and The Batman.

He’s made stellar choices throughout his career, turning a heartthrob start into a credible run of good, risk-taking roles.

The first, most critical drop will, of course, be the villain. Because, as they say in Hollywood, a movie is only ever as good as its villain. Without a dastardly supervillain for Bond to shoot his PPK at, Bond is just a red flag in a black suit. No names have yet reared their heads. But that didn’t stop us from suggesting a few candidates.

Speaking of which, Amazon has shown no indication of a plan to depart from the Bond-girl tradition. Though it’s (thankfully) likely to be handled sensitively for 2026: expect a complex and fully realized love interest who will still be in a gorgeous dress by the second act.

Margaret Qualley has emerged as the bookies’ favorite, having already taken part in a Bond-tribute dance at the 2025 Oscars. Sydney Sweeney has said she’s up for joining the franchise, though she told Variety she’d have more fun playing Bond himself.

Anya Taylor-Joy, Florence Pugh, and Zendaya have also been linked, with the obvious advantage of having worked with Villeneuve on Dune.

They were most recently played, respectively, by Ralph Fiennes, Ben Whishaw, and Naomie Harris. It’s not known whether any of them will return to the roles, though history suggests it’s likely.

Craig’s run offers a rough template, for what it’s worth (which is not much). He was unveiled in October 2005, started shooting Casino Royale the following January, and had it in cinemas by November 2006. Pattinson’s unveiling comes just a few weeks later in the calendar, so a similar timetable would see cameras rolling early next year and a potential release date around the end of 2027. Though that feels like a tight turnaround for such a gargantuan project. Amazon will want to get this right, so given how long it strung out Pattinson’s casting, 2028 seems a more likely ballpark.

Here’s a boring-but-pertinent fact: three of Daniel Craig’s five James Bond films debuted in October: Quantum of Solace, Skyfall, and Spectre. The remaining two, Casino Royale and No Time to Die, were released in November and September, respectively.

But this is, of course, guesswork—Amazon has offered no indication that it will follow the Craig timeline, or anything else about the movie’s schedule.

This is perhaps the most interesting part of the whole process. It began in February 2025, when the Broccoli dynasty (led by long-serving Bond masterminds Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson) announced they were handing over creative control to Amazon MGM Studios (which was formed when Amazon bought Bond’s parent studio in 2022).

Within hours of the announcement, Amazon’s owner, Jeff Bezos—the world’s richest man—took to X to ask the world: “Who’d you pick as the next Bond?”

This followed rumors of a rift between the two parties, with the Wall Street Journal reporting that Barbara suggested to friends that she didn't like the idea of Amazon execs taking a data-driven approach to the series, as well as the use of the word “content” in creative meetings.

Just as Disney+ has produced many Star Wars spin-offs, Amazon MGM is expected to do the same with Bond—a process that began last year with the first TV series, 007: Road to a Million, a game show hosted by Succession’s Brian Cox.

Mike Hopkins, head of Prime Video and Amazon MGM Studios, said in February 2025: “We are honored to continue this treasured heritage, and look forward to ushering in the next phase of the legendary 007 for audiences around the world.”

Secrets of Longevity, According to Nonagenarians

#6884. The 25 Best Cocktails for Thanksgiving

With a whiskey warmer, spiced sour, or comforting classic in your hand, you can get through any awkward family gathering.

Are you ready for your annual awkward family gathering? Thanksgiving is finally here, and if thinking about safe topics to discuss with all three generations of your family is making you break out in hives, then rest assured we’ve got some recipes to give you the liquid courage to face it.

You’ve got the turkey basted and roasting in the oven. You’ve got your grandmother’s perfect mac and cheese. You’ve got mashed potatoes—the best. And you’ve got store-bought pie from your slacker cousin. But, wait, do you know what you’ll be drinking for the most important feast of the year? We promise: There’s something for everyone at your dinner table on this curated list of cocktails.

Does Grandpa only abide by the saying, “If it’s brown, drink it down?” We’ve got a few whiskey drinks that will keep him on ice. Did your aunt who drinks only French reds run through the bottle she brought already? There’s a sangria recipe that’ll have her too busy sipping to snip. Need a warm cup of something strong while you “take the dog for a walk”? There’s an Irish coffee that’ll keep your wits about you. So hurry up and add these cocktail recipes to your Turkey Day menu.

Here’s a whiskey sour that makes your fall-flavor dreams come true.

Add ingredients to a shaker and shake without ice. Pour into a glass and garnish with grated nutmeg and a dried apple slice.

This twist on the old-fashioned adds an autumnal flavor that will keep this classic feeling fresh.

Combine all ingredients into a mixing glass with ice. Stir until chilled and diluted. Strain into a rocks glass with a large cube. Garnish with an orange peel studded with cloves.

The delicious spice that goes into a pie doesn’t have to be just for lattes or baked goods.

Cover the rim of a stemless wineglass in simple syrup. Dip in cinnamon sugar and add ice to the glass. Pour in pumpkin-spice liqueur and Josh Cellars sauvignon blanc. Stir, then top off the glass with seltzer.

This three-ingredient cocktail is easy to whip up but will taste like it was complicated and time-consuming.

Mix cider and cinnamon whiskey in a Champagne flute. Top with Champagne, then stir lightly until incorporated. Garnish with an apple slice.

A sweet, fizzy sipper to enjoy before dinner.

Add all liquid ingredients to a stemless wineglass. Add ice. Stir to incorporate, then garnish.

A roasty, toasty espresso martini to enjoy after the big dinner.

Shake ingredients hard with ice until frothy, about 15 seconds. Double-strain into a chilled coupe or martini glass. Garnish with coffee beans, nutmeg, and cinnamon.

This sumptuous hot cocktail makes a great Thanksgiving treat.

In a mug, dissolve the sugar cubes in a little hot water. Add rum and butter. Fill the mug with hot water. Sprinkle nutmeg on top.

The margarita isn’t just a summer drink anymore.

In a cocktail shaker, combine all the ingredients with ice and shake. Rim a glass with cinnamon sugar and serve over rocks. Garnish with slices of lime and orange.

You need to keep the booze flowing to keep the mood light and the conversation going. So mix together this sangria recipe that’ll satiate any apple-cider lover.

Place apples, orange, and cloves in a large pitcher. Drizzle honey over the fruit, then add in apple cider, rum, ginger beer, and white wine. Stir to mix. Top with ice and let chill for 20 minutes before serving. Serve in glasses, including the chunks of fruit.

Whoever invented pouring bourbon into a cup of tea, we want to give you a big hug.

Boil water in a teapot. Steep star anise pod or tea bag inside. Add bourbon, honey, lemon, and Tiki bitters to a mug. Stir. Serve in glass mugs with a spoon and the teapot. To enjoy, pour tea over cocktail base to taste and stir.

This aperitivo is the perfect refreshment before you start serving those heavy Thanksgiving dishes.

Pour Campari and sweet vermouth into a highball glass filled with ice. Top with club soda. Garnish with an orange twist.

A balanced spin on the Manhattan that uses Scotch whisky, this cocktail will impress any fan of brown liquor.

Stir the Scotch, vermouth, and bitters well with cracked ice. Strain into a chilled cocktail glass. Garnish with a lemon twist.

Simple enough to whip up in under five minutes, this cocktail has enough Scotch to knock Grandpa under the table.

Stir the Scotch and amaretto well with cracked ice in a mixing glass. Strain over fresh ice into an old-fashioned glass.

If you’re a big fan of cranberry sauce (the one that comes in a can), you can cure your cranberry craving with this vodka cocktail.

Shake ingredients in a shaker with cracked ice. Strain into a chilled cocktail glass, then garnish with lime.

This green-tinged cocktail tastes fresh and slightly tart, but it’s boozy enough to chill you out after that heavy holiday meal.

Add all ingredients into a shaker. Shake well with cracked ice. Strain into a coupe glass. Garnish with a lime wedge and Luxardo cherries.

The Black Russian is the espresso martini’s cooler, more laid-back cousin. It’s so simple to make and yet deliciously complex in taste.

Stir ingredients with ice in a mixing glass. Strain into an old-fashioned glass over fresh ice.

Sometimes you don’t want to have to choose between two options, because you want to have it all! And you can have it both ways, with this gin-and-vodka martini, the vesper.

Stir ingredients briskly with ice in a mixing tin until very cold. Strain into a chilled cocktail glass. Garnish with a large, thin-cut lemon peel.

Why not turn your holiday gathering into an international experience? Incorporate this Russian classic into your cocktail rotation.

Squeeze lime juice into a copper mug (or a Collins glass) and drop in the spent hull. Add ice cubes, then pour in the vodka and fill with cold ginger beer. Serve with a stirring rod.

This rum cocktail will bring back fond memories of warmer weather.

Fill a tall glass with ice cubes. Add rum. Pour in ginger beer and lime juice. Stir with a barspoon, then garnish with a lime wedge.

Holidays are synonymous with Champagne, so pop open a bottle of bubbly and pour out one of these delicious cocktails.

Stir rum, lime juice, and honey thoroughly with cracked ice in a chilled cocktail shaker. Pour unstrained into a Collins glass. Top with Champagne.

#6882. Senior Software Engineer II, Agentic Apps at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Creators use Kit's first-party apps and integrations to connect Kit to the rest of their business. Today we build and maintain these apps by hand, with help from an agency partner. That doesn't scale. We're looking for a Senior Software Engineer II to own app delivery and maintenance end to end, so a small team can support creators of every size.

You'll be the first engineer on the Apps squad. Your focus is shipping high quality apps at a high pace, and maintaining them to an industry-best standard. To get there, you'll take a systems-level view and build on Kit's coding agent. You'll work closely with our AI Developer Experience squad, which builds the coding agent. You'll own the creator experience of every app you build, and you're free to improve Kit's platform when that makes those apps better.

This is a full-time IC role for an AI-first, product-minded, full-stack engineer who has shipped agent workflows to production and judges them by reliable outcomes, not lines of code. You combine technical depth with leadership and extreme ownership. You take input from Partnerships, Product, and Design to help shape the "what," and you own the "how." You thrive in ambiguity, take initiative without waiting for permission, and elevate everyone around you through your generosity and optimism.

We're excited about first-party apps because they let creators do more with Kit than Kit does on its own. Our own apps should just work, and set the bar for every app built on Kit.

You'll report to John Bohannon, Senior Engineering Manager for the Ecosystem and Apps squads. You'll collaborate closely with a Partnerships Manager, Product Manager, Product Designer, and engineers on the Ecosystem squad. You'll also work closely with our AI Developer Experience squad on Kit's coding agent, and with a contractor who helps maintain our apps.

The Apps squad works as a sibling to Ecosystem. You'll set its rhythms, collaborate with our Partnerships Manager on which apps to build next, and join Ecosystem's cycle planning.

A peer onboarding buddy will help you learn our systems and codebase, and you'll have engineers across Kit for support and mentorship.

First Week: Set up your local environment and merge your first production code. Meet the team through Get To Know You's. Start the app handover with the contractor who maintains them today. Watch the recorded app walkthroughs and read the runbooks for our most-used apps.

First Month: Audit our first-party apps and integrations, starting with the apps that need the most maintenance. Measure what each app costs to maintain, including partner app reviews like those at Google, Meta, and Shopify. Learn how Kit's API, OAuth, and App Store support creators from small to large. Fix bugs and support escalations in the apps you now own. Learn how Kit's coding agent works with the AI Developer Experience squad.

First Three Months: Adapt Kit's coding agent to build your first apps and maintain existing ones. Give it the context, guardrails, and evals it needs. Turn what you learn into shared pieces every app can use, like a reusable load test, infrastructure automation, and app manifests. Write runbooks that let others work on any app. Drive each app's maintenance toward zero human time with the coding agent.

First Six Months: Ship new apps at a steady pace. Take over the rest of the app catalog and apply the coding agent to its maintenance. Show that one engineer with agents can maintain many apps at high quality. Define the metrics and dashboards for agent-built apps: launches, reliability, time to fix, and revenue impact. Share how agents build and maintain our apps, for example in a Lunch & Learn.

First Year: Kit ships more high-quality apps, faster, and the coding agent handles more of the routine builds and fixes, with you steering the system. The legacy integration model is retired. You use catalog metrics to recommend where the Apps squad invests next. You and the AI Developer Experience squad improve the coding agent together, so other Kit engineers benefit too.

Expert in agentic AI development. You've built agent workflows that ship and maintain production code, with evals, guardrails, and human review.

Strong in APIs, including OAuth, webhooks, and integrations with third-party platforms. Fluent in Ruby on Rails, or able to ramp into it fast. Full-stack, with React where an app needs it.

Ship well-tested, performant, accessible code, whether you or an agent writes it. Set the quality bar and build the tests, evals, and reviews that hold agents to it.

Set up dashboards, alerts, and load tests that find problems across many apps early.
