#6881. The 15 Most Thoughtful Personalized Gifts

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These will make shopping easier than ever this year.

It might only be November, but the holidays are just around the corner, and with Hanukkah starting early this year on December 4, I’m already collecting gifts for everyone I love. Getting ahead of the curve has its perks: fewer frantic shipping fees, for one, and enough time to find something that feels genuinely special.

The easiest way to make a gift special? Get it personalized. Because who wants to tear through wrapping paper only to find the same thing everyone else is getting this season? Go the custom route, and even a classic gift feels like it was made just for them (because, well, it was).

Think engraved ID bracelets and signet rings for the guys, birthstone bracelets and sparkly initial pendants for the women in your life, and personalized sports merch for the fan whose team is a whole personality trait. Add sentimental photo albums full of family memories and a few delightfully quirky surprises they’ll never see coming, and you’ve got a list worth getting a head start on. Here are some of our favorite personalized gift ideas for the holidays this year.

Know someone who takes great pleasure in writing hand-written letters? Make their next correspondence even more exciting with personalized writing paper from Smythson. Customize everything from the size and color of the paper, the text and motif at the top of the paper, and the tissue lining and border on the envelopes.

Engraved jewelry is one of the classiest personalized gifts you can get for someone, especially when it’s simple and refined like this ID chain bracelet from Miansai. I got this bracelet for my boyfriend with his name engraved on it for the holidays last year, and he wears it pretty much every day.

A personalized jersey is a no-brainer for anyone with a favorite sports team. The Fanatics custom shop has merch for NFL, MLB, NBA, WNBA, NHL, MLS, and even college teams, so it really has something for every kind of sports fan. And it’s not just jerseys—there’s also custom drinkware, bags, accessories, and more.

Even if your giftee isn’t super into astrology, they’ll appreciate a candle that’s just for them (or, well, anyone that shares their birthday). If they are into astrology, they’ll love all the fun facts about their birthday on the candle’s label, including their Zodiac sign, element, constellation, tarot card, and more. And all you have to know is their birthday, so it’s a pretty easy gift.

Speaking of birthdays, birthstone jewelry is another classic gift that’s personalized by design. And all you have to know is their birth month to get it right. Brilliant Earth has a great selection of birthstone jewelry, from pendant necklaces and colorful earrings to gemstone tennis bracelets and rings. You can even add multiple birthstones to this eternity lab diamond ring, so you can personalize it with the birthstones of everyone in your family.

For the one who spends their weekends on the golf course: They’ll truly impress their golf buddies with a box of personalized golf balls. And it comes with tees, a divot tool, and poker-chip ball markers so they have everything they need.

If you have the time and want to do something really special, order one of these luxury lighters from S.T. Dupont and get it engraved in person at a local engraving shop or jewelry repair store. The extra effort will show, and your giftee will have something to carry with them wherever they go for years to come.

You’ll have fun playing around with all the features in Ralph Lauren’s online custom hat maker—we certainly have. Choose the color and add a name, logo, or monogram, and the result will be a one-of-a-kind baseball cap your giftee will wear all the time.

Don’t just print out a photo and call it a day; get it printed on glass for something truly unique. Upload your photo to Fracture and personalize it even more with colorful borders, templates, frames, and stands.

Ever gone into a comic book store and seen the walls of Funko POP action figures from every franchise you can imagine? Having cute action figures of all your favorite Star Wars characters is great, but imagine how cool it would be to give someone an action figure of themself. With Funko POP, you can do just that—and you’ll have fun building it. You can even get a pair of Funko POP figures, so you can include yourself in it, too.

If you’re looking for a romantic personalized gift for your partner, surprise them with a watercolor painting of the place where you had your first date or the spot where you got engaged. Whether it was a local coffee shop, beloved movie theater, or beautiful outdoor space, it’ll look good painted above your names.

In a world where everything is online and in the cloud, a physical photo album is all the more sentimental and special. Artifact Uprising makes putting together a photo album easy, and the result is high quality and beautiful. It’s something they’ll want to display on their coffee table for everyone to see.

Gifts based on birthdays is a common theme in this gift guide, but what can we say? It’s an easy way to personalize stuff. If they’re a history buff (or just love reading the news), they’ll enjoy flipping through this customized book of newspaper headlines from their birthday through the years.

You know that person who is absolutely obsessed with their dog or cat? (Okay, it’s me.) They’ll obsess over any gift that has to do with their beloved pet, like this custom sweatshirt embroidered with the face of their dog or cat. Just upload a photo of their pet (you can probably find one on their Facebook or Instagram) and choose a sweater color and size.

A customized L.L. Bean tote bag is something they’ll use all the time. It’s one of our favorite gifts of all time, and it’s an absolute classic.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

El Niño be damned. Winter is just around the corner, and even in a very weird year for weather, I can guarantee you this: There are cold days ahead. It pays to be prepared for them. And you could do a whole lot worse, preparation-wise, than the new collaboration between Buck Mason and Sierra Designs.

You probably know both brands already, but just in case you don’t, here’s a brief primer. Buck Mason, founded in 2013, makes the kind of cool-but-accessible menswear staples that you can build an entire wardrobe around. Sierra Designs is one of the OG American outdoors brands, founded in 1965 and responsible for iconic designs like the 60/40 mountain parka and all sorts of other equipment and clothing built for exploring nature. Both, handily enough, were born in California—and today they released their first-ever collaboration.

The new collection draws heavily from Sierra Designs’ ‘70s heyday, with reworked versions of an archival down jacket and vest serving as the stars of the show. Both are made from lightweight Japanese nylon and utilize a 600-fill-power, 80/20 blend of down and feathers for insulation. They’re complemented by a range of vintage-y tees, thermals, totes, and caps.

The overall vibe is easygoing and not too techy—exactly the sort of stuff you’ll want to wear in everyday life, not just on some windswept summit. Which is what makes it so damn good. There’s no shortage of high-performance winter gear out there, but much of it falls short on the style front. This is the sort of stuff that’ll do the job but won’t have you looking like you just finished a black-diamond run. Wear it with chinos and hiking boots or jeans and sneakers. To the coffee shop on a blustery morning or the park for a day out with the family. Or, yeah, even in the great outdoors. Just make sure to snag whatever you like soon, as Buck Mason’s collaborations have a nasty habit of selling out.

#6867. The Inconvenient Truth About the Electricity AI Requires

AI companies are facing political backlash, but they’re staring down an even bigger problem as they look to take over the world.

He-Man liked to exclaim: “I have the power!” AI should be so lucky.

When it comes to the wielders of artificial intelligence, and the data centers needed to fuel it, language and numbers become inescapably extreme: trillions in investment, gigawatts of electricity, the extinction of the human race. It’s almost cartoonish, until you get down to some brass tacks: The power is not there to fuel these dreams of the would-be Masters of the Universe.

“The U.S. grid is not designed for large data-center loads,” says Anubhav Arora, senior director of global infrastructure at Fitch and a coauthor of a recent paper the ratings agency published on data centers. “Securing reliable, sufficient power is the single greatest bottleneck in data-center development,” the paper’s authors write. (Esquire and Fitch share the same parent company, Hearst.)

In the U.S.—which is by far the world leader in data centers—electricity demand from the facilities could reach up to 649 terawatt-hours (TWh) a year by 2030, according to a 2026 Berkeley Lab analysis, or roughly 12 percent of all U.S. electricity consumption. Powering New York City for an entire year requires one tenth that energy.

Meeting global demand for compute could require nearly $7 trillion in capital investment by 2030, including $5.2 trillion for AI-related data centers, according to McKinsey estimates. Estimates from Goldman Sachs Research say that Europe will need to invest more than $3.5 trillion on its grid in the next ten years to cope with increased demand. (China looks to be fine, but it is slow to the data-center boom.)

And the problem is not just a lack of juice. “The actual grid infrastructure . . . is also basically limited in terms of transmission capacity, and the interconnection process is a little bit outdated,” says Arora. That grid—the network of transmission lines, transformers, and power plants that create and distribute electricity to hundreds of millions of users—is constrained, and the interconnection backlog is massive. Put another way: Not only is the shop running low on milk, but the line to get inside is stretching around the block. “You could build a data center in one and a half to two years, but to get interconnection, it takes five to ten years,” Arora says.

According to Cleanview, a company that tracks U.S. power infrastructure, there are currently 1,454 operating data centers, as well as up to 578 under construction and 2,663 proposed. Companies like Alphabet, Meta, and Anthropic are scrambling. With that scramble, there is money to be made. PJM, the largest power-grid operator in the United States, has been trying to devise a new way to accommodate the data-center boom. Its proposed “Bring Your Own New Capacity” (or BYONC) framework would have allowed new data-center projects to buy energy in the form of a battery and add it to the grid. But in October, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission rejected that portion of PJM’s proposal, sending the grid operator back to the drawing board.

Important here is what kind of data centers we’re talking about. Of the more than 3,200 data centers proposed globally, 800 of them are hyperscalers. These are the biggies. The campuses. Sprawling centers with square footage in the millions and power demands upwards of 100 MW (which could power 100,000 homes). Collectively, some estimates put the global power usage at 200 TWh annually. (That’s juice for 11 million homes.)

How can companies even think about the grid beginning to handle that demand? Increasingly, they aren’t. Many hyperscalers are looking to “bring your own power [BYOP],” says Jonathon Smith, associate director of Sustainable Finance Research at Fitch. In other words, build their own power plants. The problem here is twofold: First, very few people on the planet know how to build something like this. You could buy the biggest army the world has ever seen; try moving it without engineers. Second, it poses very real and very dangerous environmental threats.

Recently, the journal Nature Sustainability published a study revealing that the annual carbon dioxide emissions of the largest data centers in the U.S. are equal to that of a small country, like Paraguay or Denmark. And that study looked at data centers from 2024 and 2025. Data centers have exploded since then, with as much as a 190 percent increase from 2025 to 2026.

When companies like Anthropic say, “Don’t worry, we’re not even touching the grid,” it brings up a cartoon situation more akin to The Simpsons than a Saturday-morning serial. Picture it: a giant data center, clean, cool, not an emission in sight, just humming machines and maybe a security guard. But there’s a pipe going out the back, and if you follow it as it snakes around the corner, it connects to a behemoth power plant, burning enough natural gas to power a city, with plumes of smoke assaulting the sky above. Animated, but closer to the reality of BYOP.

“From an environmental-impact perspective, most of these developers are deploying natural-gas-based assets for power, which release GHG [greenhouse gas] emissions and air pollutants,” says Smith. The cleaner alternatives are renewables (unlikely), nuclear (terrifying), and batteries (no environmental picnic, either).

Among the environmental concerns—which inevitably bleed into the social and economic ones—are these centers’ use of water, which they need in great volume to keep those humming stacks of servers cool. While many experts point to these issues as overblown, especially as data centers look to closed-loop systems (there is an initial dump of water that gets cooled and reused), the reason you won’t stop hearing about water use is because 1) it’s concerning, and 2) water is inherently political and always has been. (Traditionally, we’ve needed the stuff to feed our animals, grow our crops, and, you know, live.) Go back to the early chapters of the Bible and what’s causing strife? Water. Go back even further and some of the first wars ever recorded—4,500 years ago in ancient Sumeria—are over the stuff. And data centers have become a fast-moving political football.

A recent Gallup poll indicates that 71 percent of Americans do not want an AI data center near where they live. Those are some very big numbers. It’s important to clarify that the data centers big tech wants to build near dense populations are for cloud computing, and they make up roughly 70 percent of all data centers. While centers for AI computing and AI training are catching up, these can be built farther away, for the simple reason that we’re happy to wait a few minutes for AI to do our taxes but not for the cloud to recombobulate an email or Instagram post.

That’s still a huge number of data centers that need to be near you and me, so what will we do about it? The politicians are listening. New York State has issued a moratorium. There’s legislation limiting the centers on the table in nine other states. Gina Hinojosa, who just pummeled Greg Abbott in the Governor's race in Texas, campaigned on promises of a moratorium for permitting and construction on new data centers. Norway, the Netherlands, and Denmark are leading the charge on moratoriums and other restrictions in Europe as governments catch up with popular opinion.

Moreover, back in the U.S.A., Donald Trump has touted a “deal” with big tech on data centers, the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, supposedly protecting grids from the biggies. But never has Tip O’Neill’s maxim that “All politics is local” been more salient. More than 500 localities are forming their own moratoriums, zoning freezes, and permit pauses. Even after the midterms, how and if to move forward with data centers is a debate for states, counties, cities, townships, and any other geographic entity that makes laws, collects taxes, and breathes collective air.

As the Democrats take over Congress and data centers become every pol’s favorite new cudgel, as the European Union and member states enact regulatory frameworks (often tied to renewable energy and climate), as elected officials turn away from AI’s once sunnily vague promises, something is becoming clear: It’s humans who have the power.

#6860. Everything but the Plane: Here’s How One Brand Is Bringing Glamour Back to Travel

PS’s private terminals are the epitome of real luxury.

Imagine a trip to the airport with no lines. No lines to check in, to drop your bag, to be told your first-class ticket is the wrong first-class ticket for the first-class lounge. Now add to that no interpersonal friction. There are no snappy desk workers, no turbo TSA employees, and no graveyard-shift customs agents that give you grief.

Airlines can solve only so many issues. These are terminal problems. PS, a Los Angeles–based company operating its own luxury private terminals, is solving them. The goal is to get you as close to the charter experience as possible, all while still flying commercial. If you ask around or sit in the common areas and eavesdrop long enough, you’ll likely hear something along the lines of “I usually fly private, but when I don’t, this is what I use.”

The company has locations in Atlanta, Dallas Fort Worth, Miami, and Los Angeles, and the next order of business is expansion to the airports its clients use most—business hubs, mostly. But it started at LAX in 2017, and that’s the perfect airport for something like this. The horseshoe design and the traffic it creates, the small terminals, and the paparazzi culture do not cater to the A-list and executive frequent fliers that use it as a home airport. PS offers a way around that with on-tarmac connection from your flight to its terminal and vice versa. A chauffeur pulls you straight off the jet bridge and into a luxury fleet vehicle; that partnership is currently with BMW. If you don’t have any checked luggage, that 7 Series can (for an additional price) drive you straight to your destination within 35 miles of the airport. So if you are sitting in row 1 of first class, that’s about 50 steps between you and the i7 that takes you to your hotel. Or it can take you back to the PS terminal, which is not a lounge but acts as an über-private lounge experience.

It ain’t cheap: $1,300 per head one-way for the Salon (a shared bar area), $4,950 for a private suite (essentially an airport hotel room with butler and spa service; this price is all-inclusive), and $1,650 for that PS Direct drive-you-straight-home service. There are discounts and perks if you opt for yearly membership, but this is all on top of your ticket, folks.

And it can lean comically luxurious, with Champagne and caviar offered every time you enter—like before my 8:00 a.m. departure, to which I’m not opposed but which caught me a little off guard. There’s the jet-set marketing—tagline: The “back door” to the airport—and meant-for-the-timeline branded cocktail napkins: “You’ve arrived.”

But once you accept that PS could not afford to excuse itself from the flex-driven airport exclusivity economy, you can really admire its hospitality-inspired finishes. Its raison d’être is to get you through the airport with as little friction as possible, but it stands out in its commitment to hospitality within those confines.

For example, interior design and artwork are curated. The centerpiece artworks of each location are done in collaboration with local artists, made to fit the location they are in. The crown jewel thus far is in Miami, where PS took over the old Pan Am headquarters and flight-training academy. The mid-century building is a Miami landmark, with geometric thermal walls acting as the facades and this pinkish sandy and limestone-heavy concrete throughout. Argentinian-born ceramicist Nina Surel matched this with tile-work water features and a ceramic mural at the center of the property. Design like this, small and mostly unnoticed in the grand scheme of things, is what turns your mind back to a golden age of travel. It’s not credit card points and moneymaking schemes—though, actually, you can buy any of the non-centerpiece artworks—just beauty for the sake of beauty.

On top of that, the food and beverage program is considered and regional. The blue-corn cornbread with honey and a ricotta spread that chef Kelsey Kirwin (chefs’ names are on the menus, an indication of the atmosphere) is making at the DFW location overtook my mother’s as my all-time favorite. I would consider the signature PS martini—with (guess what) caviar-stuffed olives—worthy of a place on any hot big-city cocktail menu. And all the coffees, snacks, and water options are head and shoulders above those of both the general-population terminals and the litany of airport lounges within them.

As far as service, yes, an agent handles your party’s check-in and keeps you abreast of delays so you don’t have to open an app—but that’s to be expected. It’s more in the interim. Servers don’t hover, they listen, and they’re backed by an extensive behind-the-scenes operation: Mention that you prefer coffee, not bubbles, before a morning flight? A note is made on your profile for the next time you visit. Only ever ask for sparkling water when you fly out of LAX? They’ll know that in Atlanta. The entire operation seems to run on Guidara’s Unreasonable Hospitality, the same way all modern restaurants do. If you’re leaving Miami and mention to a business partner that you forgot to try a Cubano (on menu) and a cafecito (off menu), both will likely be delivered to your table, compliments of the house.

But every time I start to get wowed by the service details, I remember the price point and the level of clientele. That should be expected.

Standard terminals are air-side, post-security, where everything (even a fancy and expensive airline lounge) is working within those confines. Since PS operates land-side, with security the last stage of the terminal experience, it has the flexibility to be more hospitable to guests. For example, if you book a private suite, you can fly in, use it for a business meeting, and fly out. Or your parents can accompany the family to departure, hang out in the suite, and squeeze every last second with the grandkids out of your visit. It is an airport experience actually designed around the ways we interact and what matters to us when we travel. That, more than the Champagne and caviar, is the definition of luxury.

#6859. Enhance Cozy Vibes With These 8 Captivating Woody Perfumes

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Spritzing these picks feels like cuddling up in your favorite sweater.

If you thought woody perfumes were relegated to your noncommittal Hinge dates, I’m about to introduce you to a whole new world of woodsy scents that are captivating, soft, and totally addicting. I promise these are far from the strong, heady, and cloying woods that have emanated off of the men in your life.

You see, woody notes are extremely versatile. Depending on what you pair them with, they can bend into so many fragrance families. Woods add body to really ground a scent, hence why you'll typically find them at the base. But they can also make for a soft opening too. It's really all about the full composition.

Which brings me to the best woody perfumes. Don't think it's all about cedar and sandalwood perfumes, though (granted, many of our favorites contain these, of course). There are so many unique takes on woody perfumes, whether you want to evoke a cozy fireside vibe or gravitate toward earthy scents filled with herbs and spices. Cosmo editors test perfumes for a living, and we've gone ahead and compiled the absolute best woody perfumes. From soft versions for woodsy newbies to the boldest and deepest scents for extra-cold weather, everything here is vetted and truly loved by our team. Lemme show ya some of our top faves:

You’ll be a woody perfume fanatic after smelling these eight gorgeous scents.

One Cosmo editor says: “I’m not exaggerating when I say this is my most-complimented perfume of all time. (And I own over 600 perfumes, FYI.) This is sexy and spellbinding, and though it features vanilla, it’s the woody notes that make 21:50 Reverie so captivating. Soft smokey tobacco gets a juicy burst from plum and a touch of sweetness from vanilla before this turns deep and sensual with cedarwood. I’ve worn this on plenty of dates, and other men have come up to tell me how incredible I smell.”—MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY EDITOR

#6857. The Newest Apple Watch Is 25% Off for Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Swipe the Series 12 at its lowest price of the year.

It’s no secret that Apple has been dominating tech headlines this year. But between the news of its recent price hikes on MacBooks and iPads and the release of the iPhone Duo (the tech giant’s first foldable), not much fanfare has been made about the new generations of the Apple Watch. As far as we’re concerned, that’s a damn shame, because they’re well worth the upgrade—especially if you can find one on discount.

Right on cue, Amazon’s Black Friday sale is here, and with it comes record-low prices on every Apple Watch, whether you want to shave $100 off the brand-new Apple Watch Series 12, take advantage of a sub-$200 price on the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3, or score the rugged Apple Watch Ultra 4 while it’s under $700.

Below, we’re breaking down the differences between each model—and peeling back the curtain on our price-tracking prowess—so you’re all set to shop. After all, when Apple gadgets are this cheap, they tend to go like hotcakes, so don’t say we didn’t warn you when they inevitably sell out.

Commerce editor Luke Guillory argued that compared to other fitness trackers on the market, Apple was lagging behind with its lack of readiness score functionality—until now. The Apple Watch Series 12 marks the debut of the brand’s very own readiness score thanks to a revamped tracking system. Plus, the watch now measures your heart rate more frequently to deliver Heart Rate Variability (HRV) readings every five minutes. If you’re trying to pay closer attention to your health this year, it’s a smart buy, made sweeter when it’s on sale for Black Friday.

Given that this brand-new watch dropped to just $350 for October Prime Day, we were expecting more of the same for Black Friday, so this $100 discount, bringing it to just $299, is all the more impressive.

If you’re looking for Apple’s most affordable watch, we suggest turning your attention to the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3. It was actually the watch that converted Gelbart to Apple wearables in the first place, arguing that its beauty is in its simplicity.

“It's an accurate and predictive workout tracker. It counts my steps and activity and connects to my phone to play music and receive messages and calls,” he says. “If you’re new to the Apple Watch and want to dip your toes, this is where I recommend you start.” It has the same sleep tracking features, like nightly sleep score and sleep apnea notifications, but lacks the ECG app or hypertension notifications of its newer siblings. It also has an 18-hour battery life so you’ll need to charge most nights.

This $179 Black Friday deal drops the SE 3 even lower than its previous record-low price of $199, which we first saw during October Prime Day, making the most affordable Apple Watch an even smarter buy.

Our editors have loved every version of the Apple Watch Ultra thus far, and the latest model not only delivers on greatness once again, but also just hit a record-low price for Black Friday.

The Apple Watch Ultra 4 has the same new Readiness features and advanced health tracking the Series 12 has, in addition to the dive functionality, ruggedness specs, and satellite capabilities we’ve come to know and love from the Ultra line. Plus, from a sartorial standpoint, it’s hard to beat the new semi-translucent watch bands, which took design cues from the ocean.

Our philosophy when it comes to shopping for Apple tech during Black Friday? If you find the watch with the features you need at a price you can stomach, pick it up. It’s incredibly likely that the deals you see here are the best ones you’ll see for the rest of the year.

#6852. Dakota Johnson Wore Cherry Cola Nails, So Now I’m Wearing Cherry Cola Nails

A syrupy upgrade to your typical burgundy mani.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

One of my guilty pleasures is sipping on a large vanilla cherry Coke at the movie theater. I’m really not a big soda drinker, but there’s just something about the combination of a fizzy, cherry-flavored soda, a bucket of popcorn, and a good movie that does it for me. And luckily for me, I’ve recently discovered another way to indulge: cherry cola nails. Yep, my go-to concession stand order is officially making its way to my fingertips, and naturally, I’m eating (or should I say drinking?) it up.

Dakota Johnson was also spotted wearing cherry cola nails earlier this month at a screening of Flesh Impact in Los Angeles, which only further fueled my need to get the look. Similar to syrup nails, these have a glossy finish to them, but are dark, rich, and reddish-brown, just like the classic soda. It’s basically the perfect marriage of two of my favorite fall nail colors, burgundy and chocolate brown. Ahead, everything you need to know about the trend, plus plenty of inspiration for your next nail appointment. Movie theater soda optional... but highly encouraged.

Think of a Cherry Coke: It’s dark brown with touches of red when the light hits just right. And that’s exactly the goal of the manicure version. “Cherry cola nails are the perfect blend of deep burgundy, rich chocolate brown, and a hint of red,” says celebrity nail artist Jacqueline Pham. “Think of that beautiful, dark cherry soda color that looks almost brown in certain lighting but has a cherry red undertone when the light hits.”

They’re also a great way to dress up your nails for the holiday season: “It’s a little sultry, and a fun alternative to your classic burgundy red manicure,” adds Pham. “You can play around with different shades of brown and burgundy and a slight change in either color can create a completely different cherry cola effect.”

Need some ideas of how exactly to incorporate the color onto your fingertips? Firstly, Pham suggests wearing them as a single-color mani—glossy and straightforward with a classic, high shine finish. “It’s timeless, elegant, and looks beautiful on both short and long nails,” she says. She also likes adding a little shimmer too, which you can do with the help of Essie’s Gel Couture Liquid Diamonds in Size Me Up or Color Street’s Nail Strips in Velvet Dahlia. If you’re craving some nail art, you could also do a twist on a classic French manicure with cherry cola tips over a sheer nude base.

First things first, you’ll want to reach for more than one bottle of nail polish. “The secret to achieving the perfect cherry cola shade is layering your colors rather than using just one,” says Pham. “I personally recommend starting with a rich chocolate brown as your first coat, followed by a deep burgundy as your second coat.” This mimics a cherry soda too, with a brown base and a hint of cherry flavor and color. “The brown creates a warm base, while the burgundy adds cherry tint,” explains Pham. After applying a base coat, she says to do “one thin coat of chocolate brown and one thin coat of burgundy, allowing each layer to dry.” After those two coats, she says to “finish with a high gloss top coat to give it that glassy, soda-like shine.”

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6849. Couples Need to Stop Being So Weird About GTA 6

Grand Theft Auto VI came out last Thursday, and somehow the most unbearable thing about it isn't the grown men playing the game—it's the couples.

After the release of the game, my feed became a nonstop slideshow of printed contracts, handmade gift baskets, and girlfriends narrating their boyfriends' disappearance into Vice City like it's a deployment. Couples posted printed agreements that grant the player 10 full days of uninterrupted access.

I'm not here to tell anyone not to play a video game. People should do what makes them happy. But why are we all being so weird about a video game?

Ten days off from the relationship, so a man can play a game. I'd like to meet the woman who has ever been granted a 10-day leave from her partner, her family, her job, and the emotional labor of every relationship in her life. Women don't get to just disappear to pursue their hobbies.

Some women in relationships are putting together “offerings” for their partner. One viral example involved a GTA-themed gift basket stocked with snacks and drinks for the player's sessions. Another woman posted a Target-run basket of her boyfriend's favorite snacks with the caption, "This is your sign to make your man a GTA 6 basket!"

No. It's not. When was the last time he made you a basket for one of your hobbies?

On the flip side, some men are gifting their partners a basket, with the idea that women need something in return—a little distraction to keep busy while he's busy. Women are not toddlers who need to be handed a snack and a coloring book while the grown-ups play. We have friends. We have jobs. We have entire lives that existed before his Rockstar account and will exist after it. We'll be fine. We might even enjoy the quiet!

It all begs the question: Why can't men just have hobbies like normal people? Women have hobbies all the time. We go to pilates, we read, we get absorbed in a show, and nobody drafts a legal document. Let’s keep up that energy. Just play the game and also make time for the rest of your life!

But it isn’t just about people finding the time to play the game. There’s separate discourse among couples about the relationship players maintain with Lucia, the main female character.

Online, some women have expressed feeling jealous of Lucia. Around the game's lead-up, users on TikTok and Instagram were claiming that boyfriends who nurture a virtual relationship with Lucia could be considered cheating. A YouGov poll of 4,000 adults found around 6 percent would consider dating in games like GTA VI to be infidelity. That's a generally small number, but it's still making headway on social media.

Lucia is a character. She is pixels. If your relationship can be undone by a woman who exists only to rob a gas station with your boyfriend, the problem was never Lucia.

To be fair, some women have leaned into the joke. One girlfriend gifted her boyfriend the game with a four-rule counter-contract that ended with the line "Lucia is not your real girlfriend." I respect the bit—but it still icks me out.

On the other hand, there have been people claiming that GTA 6 is teaching men to be better partners. I’d like to politely disagree. The theory goes that watching Jason treat Lucia well, and putting effort into that relationship on screen, will make players better boyfriends in real life.

I'm glad they're being attentive to Lucia. I mean that. It’s a nice change to see the video game encouraging men to be decent human beings (though the bar is low). But at the end of the day, they're buying flowers, fixing things up, and doing the emotional work for a woman who doesn't exist, while their actual girlfriends are across the room eating a basket of Swedish Fish.

Can we all get real for a second? What grown man needs a video game to teach him how to treat a partner? Showing up, communicating, and putting in effort are not secret skills only unlocked by a video game. I promise you your boyfriend is not learning valuable relationship skills playing GTA 6.

So to any couples out there being particularly weird about GTA 6: play the game, enjoy it, and please just be normal. Be a person with a hobby. It really isn't that deep.

#6848. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

#6844. Why Do We All Love a Small-Town Holiday Romance? Guarding Stars Might Have the Answer

The fantasy of falling in love in a small town has surprisingly little to do with the small town itself.

I’ll find any excuse to cozy up with a holiday rom-com, so when Guarding Stars dropped on Netflix, it was a no-brainer. Gossip Girl and Supernatural alumni Leighton Meester and Jared Padalecki are embracing one of my favorite tropes: the good old “small-town holiday romance.” Leighton’s character Hannah goes with Jared’s character Jack to his hometown pretending to be his girlfriend, while actually serving as his bodyguard. It got me thinking: Whether we’re die-hard city dwellers or seasoned travelers, why do we still love the home-for-the-holidays romance trope?

For me, the answer lies in how this specific genre taps into our own deep-rooted desire for escapism. We’ve seen these kinds of fake girlfriend or friends-to-lovers storylines play out a million times before, so their predictability is incredibly comforting to watch. We have, quite literally, seen this film before. And during the heightened chaos of the holiday season, it places viewers in the safe bubble many people crave.

Plus, the small-town setting in Guarding Stars is perfectly cozy. The two head to Jack’s obnoxiously large family home in Montana that’s been pristinely decorated in Nancy Meyers–inspired aesthetics. And much like in one of my favorite holiday movies, The Family Stone, the house itself is just part of the fantasy. The idea of being welcomed into a close-knit community with acceptance might be exactly what we dream of the holidays being like. Being loved by a family that, in the case of Hannah, is not even your own is really intoxicating.

Even the dating looks more fun. Hannah and Jack spend the whole movie dating their way around town, doing cutesy romantic things like attending the local rodeo, getting caught on a kiss cam, and mucking out horses together in the family stables. These wouldn’t be your go-to date locations in a more urban, city-based setting, so their dating experience feels playful and different from everyday life.

And Guarding Stars finds a way to elevate this trope. Hannah isn’t actively looking for a man when all of this unfolds. She’s just doing her job as a bodyguard when she inadvertently falls into a snowball of festive shenanigans. Hannah is doing fine on her own, but there’s something sweet about watching her let her guard down. Guarding Stars gently suggests that by Hannah making herself a little more vulnerable, she’s making room for more people to love and support her. As someone who tends to handle the hard stuff alone (eldest daughter syndrome, anyone?), that really resonated with me.

So often, this genre leans toward women going to the small town and being “fixed” or changed by the relationship they encounter. Their lives in the big city are unsatisfying, actually, and they really would rather work at the Christmas tree farm. That doesn’t happen here. She ends up back in New York City, and that’s where the final action set piece of the movie happens.

Guarding Stars shows that the small-town holiday romance trope can change and evolve without losing its festive magic, because it’s about comfort and connection. We can be cozy and keep our apartments in New York City, and I’m thankful for that this holiday season.

#6843. Amazon’s Black Friday MacBook Deals Are Pretty Impressive

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Considering the price of RAM, we’ll gladly take $500 off a new MacBook.

If there’s one Apple product line where the recent price hikes hit the hardest, it’s the MacBook. Rising RAM and storage costs have pushed laptop prices up across the board, which makes scoring a new MacBook on sale all the more mission critical.

As luck would have it, Black Friday deals on Apple tech have landed on Amazon, with up to 20 percent off MacBooks—including the brand-new M6 MacBook Pros on sale for the first time ever. Given that these prices have reached record highs lately, these discounts are certainly nothing to sneeze at. In fact, now that the MacBook pricing status quo has been reset, we’d say this is probably as low as MacBook prices will get these days.

Of course, not all MacBooks were created equal. While the standard MacBook Air is great for most, those who are planning on running hardcore software might benefit from a beefed-up MacBook Pro. And if budget is your primary concern, we’re of the opinion that the MacBook Neo is a worthy buy, even if it’s only $50 off right now.

The best MacBook Air deal you’ll find right now is this $300 discount on the 15-inch model. With this laptop, you’ll get Apple’s M5 chip in a fanless, 3.3-pound frame that handles heavy multitasking and light video editing without breaking a sweat. Base storage doubled to 512 GB this year—a great upgrade for storing more apps, photos, and files—and the 15.3-inch Liquid Retina display is sharp and vivid. You also get a 12 MP Center Stage camera that’s great on video calls, Wi-Fi 7 for fast downloads, and up to 18 hours of battery, enough for a busy workday and then some.

If you’d prefer a smaller laptop, 13-inch models are up to 19 percent off. They’ve got everything the 15-inch models have, just in a more portable package. The rule of thumb for choosing between a Pro and an Air? The slimmer Air won’t replace a MacBook Pro for serious creative work or gaming, but for school, business, and general use, it’s the one to get.

#6841. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

#6837. A Case for Being Single During the Holidays

valentine’s Day is the official holiday for couples—so why does the fall and winter holiday season feel like a love and relationship fest?

This time of year has literally been deemed “cuffing season”: You’re encouraged to take couples pictures at a pumpkin patch, bring a date to Thanksgiving, drink hot cocoa by the fireplace at Christmas, and have someone special to kiss at midnight on New Year’s Eve.

But even though we’ve been inundated with countless signs that this is the best time to couple up, I’m going to go out on a limb here and say that I actually think there’s no better time than the holiday season to be single.

“The holidays can actually be a great time for creating our own traditions and sense of self,” relationship expert Bethany Nicole tells Cosmopolitan. “Being single over the holidays gives us a much-needed time of rest, reflection, and recalibration.”

So instead of falling victim to the pressures of coupling up during the holidays, why not just lean into being single instead? You might even find yourself enjoying the holidays more. Why? Thought you’d never ask:

According to Newsweek, 65 percent of Americans admitted that they’re stressed about the amount of money they’ll spend during the holidays. And for 73 percent of them, this financial stress actually makes the holidays less enjoyable. Can you guess who people are spending most of this money on? That’s right: their children and their romantic partners.

Sure, you might still have to get your parents, siblings, and your weird aunt a holiday gift—but isn’t it satisfying knowing that you don’t have to budget a huge chunk of change for yet another person in your life? By staying single during the holidays, you’re actually keeping your pockets happier.

The holidays are a time to spend with loved ones. But with so many events centered around the holidays, you might be spending more of that precious time with someone you’re just getting to know instead of with your friends and family. Assuming you like your friends and family, this is not the best ratio!

When you’re single, you can spend the holiday season with whoever you want. You can go to endless friendsgivings without having to drag along a plus-one, you can eat Thanksgiving dinner comfortably with your family without having to check on someone else, and it’s actually really fun to go out on New Year’s Eve with your best friends.

On the other side of the coin, family time in general is not for the weak. I get that, because mine isn’t either. If you were in a relationship, you’d probably be panicking right now about how to explain to your partner that your aunt is really funny if you ignore that she hates your mother or that your dad salutes the turkey before he cuts into it. But when you don’t have a date to Thanksgiving, you can just enjoy the weird and disturbing quirks of your family without having to explain them away to your partner.

When you’re coupled up, you’re expected to be “selfless” and “generous.” Ew. When you’re single, on the other hand, you can just be your normal, selfish self—and I truly think that’s beautiful.

Someone with a partner would probably be expected to give up the last piece of the pumpkin pie or be giving with an extra big piece of turkey. But because you aren’t in a relationship, you can have as much pie as you want—without pretending like you’re not picking up the speed while eating so your partner doesn’t get more bites than you.

Being around your own family for the holidays is torture enough—imagine if you then had to add a partner’s family to the mix? Not just that, but you’d most likely have to pretend to like them, too. And even get to know them!

Meeting an S.O.’s friends and family is a lot of pressure. Even though the holidays are a time of joy and celebration, they’re also super stressful. And you just don’t need the added stress of trying to impress your boyfriend’s dad, who doesn’t believe women should be in leadership positions.

Sure, having a partner around the holidays is nice—doing romantic things with someone you care deeply about can really add to the experience. But if you really want a partner, you have the rest of the new year to try and find someone.

For now, I say save your money, hang out with your friends, and do what the holidays were intended for: eating pie until you’re sick.

#6834. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

OpenAI has announced what it says is an AI-generated solution to the Navier-Stokes existence and smoothness problem, one of mathematics' seven Millennium Prize Problems, while Anthropic has reported separate advances in research mathematics and formal proof generation.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

#6831. 32 Best White-Elephant Gifts Under $50 That’ll Actually Get Stolen Twice

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Budget-friendly bangers and cheap crowd-pleasers for every chaotic holiday swap.

White-elephant gift exchanges are fun, but first you have to come up with the perfect gift everyone will want—within the established budget. Need a refresher on the game? Here’s how the chaos unfolds: Everyone brings a present, draws a number, and then, going in order based on which numbers everyone drew, either unwraps a mystery gift or steals one that’s already been revealed.

Some common caveats are that there’s a cap on steals per item (usually two or three), you can’t immediately steal back what was just swiped, and your turn ends when you open your gift (or exchange it for an already-opened one). The final player gets the power position—and sometimes a last swap—depending on house rules. You might also hear the game called Dirty Santa: same energy, same drama, whether it’s with coworkers or cousins.

What to bring under, say, $50? (That’s a pretty nice price ceiling.) Go funny but functional, like the stocking stuffers people will actually use. Think cozy comforts, clever kitchen gadgets, mini games, candles that actually smell good, and little indulgences that feel pricier than they are. Skip anything weirdly personal—you want universal hits that sisters, brothers, moms, dads, friends, and coworkers can all fight over with a smile. Bonus points if it’s something you’d happily steal back yourself.

That’s the general idea. It’s like Secret Santa but evil. The best part of all is that cheap is the way to go. A budget of $50 is more than enough, and there’s a lot of shit out there. So we sorted through it all and found the best white-elephant gifts for your 2026 festivities.

This mini Zen garden will bring balance and serenity to their desk, either at home or at work.

#6829. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

#6825. This Forgotten ’90s Comedy Is the Most Demented Thanksgiving Movie Ever

Ed O’Neill and Ethan Embry go on the road in Dutch but end up in some bizarre and disturbing places.

Let’s start off by acknowledging that the late John Hughes wrote and directed the greatest of all Thanksgiving movies with Planes, Trains and Automobiles. There are definitely other great ones, but … Dutch is a different kind of creature. This is a Thanksgiving family comedy in which a grown man fights, kidnaps, and abandons a child. Occasionally he holds him at gunpoint. It’s a rotten child but a kid nonetheless. The movie still made me laugh, but it was mostly in disbelief.

This road-trip saga that Hughes penned and produced in 1991 is by the numbers in most ways but excels in its brazen wrongness, even by the free-range standards of Generation X. I come here to praise this messy movie, not to bury it. It’s a relic from when films could be wilder, shaggier, and more shocking than the rigidly tame family comedies of today. Dutch is many things, but it is not refined to baby-food levels of inoffensive smoothness.

Hughes didn’t direct this one, handing off those duties to Peter Faiman, who had worked extensively in Australian TV and only made one other feature film: 1986’s Crocodile Dundee. I can’t help but feel this was an experiment gone wrong, or maybe a first draft that needed another pass from Hughes. It’s built out of leftovers from better road-trip movies, and like any Frankenstein’s monster, this one runs straight into a wall and ultimately falls apart.

But if you want something different for your Thanksgiving watch, go find this one. Fair warning: It’s not streaming anywhere in the United States, so you’ll have to seek it out on a sketchy Russian bootleg site that makes it look like the footage was ripped from a worn-down VHS.

Dutch stars Ed O’Neill as the title character, a blue-collar guy sent to pick up his girlfriend’s blue-blooded son from a Georgia boarding school and deliver him to Chicago in time for the holiday feast. The not-so-surprising catch is that the kid (played by a 13-year-old Ethan Embry) mercilessly despises Dutch, and honestly the feeling is mutual.

Doyle is one of the most hateful kids in comedy history. Even the ginger demon of Problem Child was more cartoonishly mischievous than sociopathic. Embry, who was then credited as Ethan Randall, isn’t playing a troublemaking scamp like the kid in O. Henry’s “The Ransom of Red Chief” or Hughes’s own Home Alone. He’s a smug sociopath with a violent mean streak.

We learn that Dutch is a salt-of-the-earth cousin to O’Neill’s uncouth Al Bundy from Married with Children because he flicks the caviar off his cracker at a fancy party. (“I make it a rule never to eat anything a fish deposits in a riverbed.”) Okay, fair.

Compare that to how we meet Doyle: Another young boy in his boarding school comes to his room to say he has a call on the dormitory’s community phone line. “Someone from any sort of decent background would knock,” Embry’s character hisses. He then corrects the kid’s vocabulary and threatens to report him to the school administrators for mild cursing. So he’s a snob, a prick, and a rat. But even this is within the bounds of film comedy. You could picture an older version of him as a stuck-up villain in Caddyshack or Animal House.

Then there’s another, even more disturbing phone scene when Doyle gets the bad news that his rich father won’t be helping him get out of this road trip with Dutch. He hangs up and stalks past an elderly janitor who is sweeping the hallway, silently kicking over the garbage can to scatter trash across the helpless old man’s feet. There’s no joke. He’s just a son of a bitch.

Doyle would fit in great with the Children of the Corn, except he’d scowl at their shabby farm clothes.

Obviously this kid is going to get some form of comeuppance. When Dutch shows up at his dorm room to start the trip, Doyle beats him with a golf club and shoots him in the testicles with a BB gun. Something about this is much more violent and visceral than the Looney Tunes–style pratfalls and clobberings of Home Alone.

They have a fistfight in the tiny room, and Dutch seizes the weapon. Does he toss it away or break it? No—he uses it himself to hold the kid at gunpoint. We have just crossed beyond the boundaries of comedy, never to return. Dutch then ties the boy up, lashes him to a hockey stick for easier carrying, and stuffs underwear in his mouth to muffle his cries.

You know a movie is going off the rails when watching it makes you feel like you should be testifying against the characters as a witness for the prosecution.

So begins the drive, with Dutch and Doyle doing their best to annoy each other verbally. Then comes a bizarre scene in which Dutch stops for fireworks for some reason and sets them off in a snowy field while the kid watches from the car. I suppose he’s trying to inspire a little playfulness in the boy, but it just seems like Dutch has lost it and is indulging a pyromaniac streak.

It goes wrong and he sets his coat on fire, which makes it harder to understand why he loses his temper when the boy flicks the butt of his lit cigar at him. The man just went mano a mano with a whizzing fireworks spinner, but this is what lights his fuse?

Dutch does the reasonable thing. He abandons the boy in the snowy night and tells him to walk the rest of the way to the motel. “About 50 miles down the road!” Fifty miles? Okay, Dutch, I believe we’ve added another felony to the charges.

This isn’t one of those moments where the grown-up pulls over just beyond the tree line and waits for him. Dutch actually goes to the hotel, although he occasionally puts down his paperback and checks the window to see if the boy has made it there alive. When Doyle finally does show up, shivering and wet, Dutch says, “Here’s something you should know about me. I don’t fool around. You piss me off, I react.”

Uncle Buck certainly had its off-color and irresponsible moments, but even that havoc-prone babysitter might have called Child Protective Services over this situation.

Doyle responds by stealing Dutch’s car keys while he sleeps and parking the sedan sideways in the snowy road, where it is slammed into by an 18-wheeler. This is a comedy, so the hapless truck driver is not killed on impact.

Faiman shoots all of this with too much gritty realism. Maybe it would work if it was all a little zanier and over the top (think of Vacation), but Dutch just plays less like a series of set pieces and more like an array of crime scenes. They don’t call anyone for help because Dutch doesn’t want to admit to his girlfriend how badly things are going, so they hitchhike the rest of the way and stay in a homeless shelter, where the rotten kid learns that a lot of people have it much worse than he does.

At one point, they are picked up by a pair of pretty young women who are driving home from a job at the Superdome in New Orleans. “We’re call girls,” one of them explains. Hoo boy. Here we go.

But thankfully—mercifully—Dutch goes no further than showing the kid sleeping in the backseat with his head resting on the chest of one of the women. Not great, but it could be worse. (Worse is when Dutch tries to bond with the child over a deck of naked playing cards.)

Even Dutch seems to recognize that “prostitute road trip” probably isn’t the ideal situation for a young boy. But he explains it in the most vulgar way possible: “You and I are riding in the backseat from now on, because what that missile twister can teach you, you don’t need to learn until you’re in prison.” Then the ladies drive off without them, taking Dutch’s wallet and leaving the pair stranded again.

They spend the next leg of their trip riding in the open concrete pipes in the back of a long-haul truck, but they fall asleep and wake up in a trailer depot where they are roughed up by two security guards. Doyle responds by drawing his BB gun on them and making them believe he is going to blow their heads off.

This same joke was deployed by Chevy Chase in the Hughes-scripted National Lampoon’s Vacation (“Are you kidding, this is a Magnum P.I.”) but here, again, it just plays as psychotic violence rather than absurd humor. “The kid is crazy,” Dutch tells the men as they beg for their lives.

By the time the duo finally get home for the Thanksgiving meal, the hostility between Dutch and the kid has softened into a grudging respect. I know: Surprise! Back at the mother’s house, Dutch physically assaults the boy’s callous father, leaving a mark with his pinkie ring on the jerk’s forehead. Instead of an assault charge, it all ends happily ever after with a callback to an earlier gag: Dutch pulls the BB pistol from his pocket and says it’s time to get the revenge he promised he would one day seek. As the boy’s smile fades, Dutch aims the gun at him—and us (!!!)—as the film cuts to the credits.

Oh, Dutch … you dangerously inappropriate maniac. Don’t ever change. And, well, he doesn’t, although the kid goes through the expected metamorphosis to become a more sensitive and understanding human.

They don’t make ’em like this anymore, as the saying goes, and I actually think that’s a pity. Dutch isn’t all that funny, but neither is toothless humor that’s overly safe. I’d gladly tolerate a few misfires like this if it meant more truly unhinged comedies like we had in the ’80s, ’90s, and early 2000s. Dutch stands as a throwback to when small-scale comedies weren’t afraid to take big swings—even if they occasionally landed against someone’s skull or testicles.

#6823. Blade Runner 2099 Is a Beautiful Slog Through a Grim Future

The new Prime Video series picks up way, way after Denis Villeneuve’s sequel. Not that it matters.

Crowded streets. Long, cool coats. Flying cars darting through smog and neon skyscrapers. An abundance of Japanese aesthetics that imply America’s lost superpower status. Ridley Scott’s seminal 1982 masterwork Blade Runner (itself adapted from Philip K. Dick’s Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep?) laid the foundation for almost all of cyberpunk science fiction, so much so that everything else feels undeservedly derivative. Even a new Blade Runner. The new series Blade Runner 2099 looks the part, but it lacks the soul of its predecessors, leaving its perspectives on what it means to be more human than human hollow, like a Replicant without memories of its own.

Streaming now on Prime Video (and just in time for a long holiday weekend) is Blade Runner 2099. A stand-alone continuation of both Ridley Scott’s towering original and Denis Villeneuve’s remarkable legacy sequel, the series takes place in 2099 (decades after Villeneuve’s Blade Runner 2049). An unseen revolution has elevated Replicants to the ruling class of Los Angeles, rendering flesh-and-bone meat-bag humans as the new discriminated minority.

Hunter Schafer flexes some of her budding leading-lady chops as Cora, a real human posing as a Replicant to smuggle herself and her brother out of Los Angeles. Her ruse leads her to Olwen (Oscar-winner Michelle Yeoh), a Replicant Blade Runner spending the final days of her artificial life span seeking out someone from her past. A humorless buddy-cop mystery, Blade Runner 2099 is mired in a plot-centric narrative that cares less about the why of all things—an underrated and beautiful feature of its predecessors—than the what and how.

In other words, it’s the first time a piece of Blade Runner media actually zeroes in on the plot, instead of anything else more interesting. Try not to think of this as an eight-hour Blade Runner series but rather an eight-hour series stretching at the seams to feel worthy of its branded title.

Is it because streaming TV in 2026 has become so plastic and hollow that even Blade Runner 2099 feels like an unengaging slog? Or were the direction of both Scott and Villeneuve so immaculate that we were distracted from how turgid they might have been all this time? Regardless, 2099’s lack of auteurship is its most deafening shortcoming. The series has been steered to the screen by executive producer Silka Luisa, whose warmer color palettes—based on her choice of more sunshine, which glistens off surfaces—lend 2099 some identity. Some.

But beyond even that minor distinction, Blade Runner 2099 is still just a plot-y crime drama that checks off boxes and goes through the motions of its genre, with nary a real thing to say. About us, about our fragile humanity, about the demise of all we’ve built through artificial intelligence. Blade Runner 2099 is a dystopian sci-fi in all the ways you expect it, but it’s hardly a good one. Sure, the original movies were oppressive, dreary, and morbid too. But they possessed a knowing glare into our fears about the future and our imminent collapse as a civilized species. The new series lacks this same insight, or indeed any of its own, all in the name of goosing up engagement numbers for the closest thing to a real-life Tyrell Corp. (Do shareholders dream of electric quarterly reports?)

Despite surface-level similarities to the movies, plus a moody noir-like motif with a sexy saxophone that, admittedly, completely and unapologetically rips, the plodding Blade Runner 2099 is a needless walkabout through a world that never begged for one. Needless is a good word, actually. The show’s strong visual execution is negated by its inability to rise to the occasion as a worthwhile or enlightening probe of the human experience, or even just as an entertaining cop show. It may be smart, but what good is intelligence if there isn’t an ounce of grace?
