#6820. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

OpenAI has announced what it says is an AI-generated solution to the Navier-Stokes existence and smoothness problem, one of mathematics' seven Millennium Prize Problems, while Anthropic has reported separate advances in research mathematics and formal proof generation.

Cybersecurity companies Rubrik and CrowdStrike have announced an expanded identity-security integration designed to move compromised identity environments from threat detection through clean recovery with less manual intervention.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

#6817. You Don’t Need Designer Sneakers. You Need These $95 German Army Trainers.

We must admit that these ones from Quince are damn good.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

In my life, I lean toward luxury and secondhand clothing wherever possible, desperately trying to get away from all the cheap garbage being sold to me on Instagram ads and the Amazon homepage. I’ve spent years of my life hunting sales and eBay listings for clothes and boots. I’m like an edge-habitat pine growing in the direction of the radiant sun that is well-made clothing, trying to forget the fast fashion slop that lies in the forest behind me.

But I throw that out the window when it comes to sneakers. Designer sneakers are, for me, one of the dumbest things I could ever buy. Even when they are made with real leather, you are paying for a plastic sole that has a lifespan of a few years. I don’t think you should pay more than the $200 price of a pair of New Balance when it comes to sneakers.

Under that price point, I’ve never been able to find a good pair German Army Trainers, which I have always wanted. That changed when I found these. Quince (yeah yeah, I was just complaining about fashion slop, but life is full of contradictions) makes a perfect pair of GATs—and it makes them for $95. I honestly think every man in the world should buy a pair of these.

This is my real qualm with designer sneakers, and it totally undercuts anything you could tell me about the second-hand sneaker hype market. What you should pay for, when you pay expensive prices for clothing, is craftsmanship. When you buy a Gucci 1953 loafer or Loro Piana cashmere you probably arrived there because of the brand, but you are paying for the craftsmanship. I see no such correlation in designer sneakers, even the ones those brands make.

But a $95 pair of kicks? That’s honest. Even though I’m not bullish on Quince’s fast fashion take on quiet luxury (nor am I bullish on its caviar program), and the fact that Quince chose to closely replicate the Margiela GAT instead of the original German army models, I will let it slide. At $95, there are no claims being made about leather quality, or the footbed, or whatever is going on in the sole you can’t see. They look good and are easy to replace; it’s about as honest of a deal as you can find shopping these days.

The thing about the original BW Sport GATs—which you can get for about this price still, though used workout sneakers aren’t my cup of tea—is that they’re just about bulletproof. Those original mid-century designs made were first issued in the 1970s and stopped being issued around the end of the Cold War, a real feat of durability.

These shoes mimic that Army-issued durability. I’ve worn these sneakers for going on a year now. In that time, I’ve seen many pairs of more expensive kicks start to fall apart, get scuffed beyond recognition, and stink like hell. These have not. The pair I’ve worn all year is the pair pictured in this story. Nothing I say in this endorsement could be more convincing than that.

And really that’s the argument here. When I wrote about the Chuck Taylors I normally wear, it was a use-it-and-lose-it argument—they cost $45, who cares if the soles get holes within 12 months. But here, I fear I’m going to sound like a marketing mouthpiece for Quince. These shoes are $95, but they’ll last just as long as a pair that cost you $950. That’s all you can ask for these days.

#6813. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

#6810. Your Vagina Has a Party Trick, and It's Called Pompoir

Meet the sex technique that puts your pelvic floor muscles to work.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

If you have a vagina (or happen to be particularly fond of them), there's a decent chance you've heard of Kegels. You know, those pelvic floor exercises that involve squeezing and releasing muscles you might not think about all that often, but the second someone mentions them, you immediately start clenching (like right now).

But what if you could put those same muscles to work during sex? That's where pompoir comes in, using different rhythms and contractions to create new sensations during penetration, sometimes without moving your hips at all.

Sometimes referred to as vaginal gymnastics, pompoir takes things a step further than your standard Kegel routine. “Think of Kegels as going to the gym and pompoir as playing the sport,” says Karen Toubi, DO, a board-certified OB/GYN and menopause and vulvovaginal disorders specialist at Lavida Women's Health, Beverly Hills.

But before you start treating your vagina like it's training for the Olympics, there's something worth knowing: Pompoir isn't about permanently tightening your vagina, performing for your partner, or guaranteeing better orgasms. It's another way to explore pleasure, provided you're doing it safely and actually enjoying yourself.

Here's how pompoir works, how to try it, and what to know before giving it a go.

Unlike regular Kegels, pompoir is less about individual squeezes and more about controlling the timing, rhythm, and intensity of those contractions during sex. The idea is to create different sensations around a partner's penis or sex toy without relying entirely on thrusting.

The muscles doing the work are the same ones you engage during Kegels, including a group called the levator ani, which helps support your pelvic organs. And while you might be able to squeeze those muscles on command, getting them to cooperate in different rhythms takes a little more finesse. “Control comes with practice, like any motor skill,” says Liz Miracle, a pelvic floor physical therapist and head of clinical quality and education at Origin.

As for what it feels like? The person practicing pompoir might notice an internal pulsing sensation, while their partner could feel anything from a gentle squeeze to a fluttering movement. Of course, everyone's experience is different, and some partners might barely notice the contractions at all.

Before trying pompoir during sex, it helps to get familiar with your pelvic floor muscles on your own. Here's where to start.

Lie on your back with your knees bent and imagine you're trying to stop yourself from peeing or passing gas. Feel that gentle squeeze and lift? That's your pelvic floor doing its thing. Just don't actually practice by stopping your pee midstream, since that can interfere with normal bladder emptying.

“Your glutes, thighs, and belly should stay quiet,” says Miracle. Focus on squeezing and fully releasing the muscles without holding your breath. If you're struggling to figure out whether you're doing it correctly, a pelvic floor physical therapist can help.

Once you've identified the right muscles, try changing up the rhythm. Dr. Toubi suggests holding a contraction for up to 10 seconds before fully relaxing, or trying quick squeezes followed by an immediate release. You can also play around with different patterns, like two slow contractions followed by a faster one.

But don't focus solely on how hard you can squeeze. Amanda Neri, PT, DPT, pelvic health expert at Smile Makers Collection, emphasizes that learning to fully relax your pelvic floor between contractions is just as important as strengthening it. Otherwise, you risk creating unnecessary tension.

According to Bryony Lees, sex and relationships educator at Little Leaf Agency, missionary, cowgirl, and spooning can all be good positions for practicing pompoir, especially if you can keep your hips relatively still. Choose whichever feels most comfortable, and have your partner stay relatively still while you experiment with different squeezes and releases.

And yes, tell your partner what you're doing. Ask what feels good, pay attention to your own sensations, and don't feel like you need to master some elaborate routine to make it worthwhile. If anything hurts, stop.

It can, depending on the person. For the person practicing it, pompoir may increase body awareness and make certain sensations more noticeable. Some people also report stronger orgasms, since the pelvic floor muscles are involved in climaxing.

“For some people, yes, it can improve sex. It isn’t a guaranteed upgrade, though, and lots of people have great sex without ever thinking about this,” says Miracle. While research suggests pelvic floor muscle training can improve sexual function, pompoir specifically hasn't been studied enough to say whether it delivers the same benefits.

And despite what you might have heard, tighter doesn't automatically mean better. In fact, an overly tense pelvic floor can make sex painful. As Dr. Toubi explains, pompoir is about developing control, not permanently tightening your vagina or proving how good you are in bed. Reminder: Sex isn't a performance review.

Generally, yes, but that doesn't mean you should spend every spare minute squeezing your pelvic floor muscles. Overdoing it can lead to muscle tension, pelvic pain, urinary urgency, and even painful sex.

“A healthy pelvic floor needs to relax as well as squeeze,” says Dr. Toubi. If your muscles are already overly tense, adding more contractions could actually make things worse.

If you experience pain during sex, have vaginismus, chronic pelvic pain, or symptoms of pelvic organ prolapse, check with a pelvic floor physical therapist before trying pompoir. The same goes for anyone experiencing urinary leakage or other pelvic floor symptoms. If you've recently given birth or had pelvic surgery, talk to your healthcare provider about when it's safe to start.

Ultimately, pompoir should be something you try because you're curious and it feels good, not because you think your vagina needs improving. There are plenty of other ways to have great sex; no vaginal workouts required.

#6806. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

#6800. Omega and Swatch Just Launched a New MoonSwatch—and No Two Are the Same

The Mission to Earthphase – Silver Snowflake, the 39th watch in the series, will have collectors keeping an eye on the Swiss weather forecast.

By MoonSwatch standards, it’s been a quiet year. Swatch has been otherwise occupied with the Audemars Piguet Royal Pop, the May release for which people lined up for six days in New York, police deployed tear gas in France, and which, according to Swatch Group, has generated more than 25 billion views on social media.

Not that interest in the MoonSwatch has gone away.

An estimated 4.5 million have been sold since the collaboration began in March 2022 (and that figure only takes us to the end of 2024). In July this year, some 1.2 million people applied to buy one of the 1,969 examples of its $570 Mission to the Moon 1969, a watch that incorporated 11 grams of 18-carat gold recycled from vintage Omega components.

The latest MoonSwatch—only the third new model this year, compared with 11 in 2023—is the Mission to Earthphase – Silver Snowflake.

An all-black counterpart to last winter’s all-white Cold Moon edition, it takes as its starting point the idea that no two snowflakes are alike.

Swatch has developed a digital process capable of generating millions of different snowflake patterns, one of which is laser-engraved onto the moonphase indicator of each watch. So, unusually for a mass-produced quartz watch, every example is unique.

It also features the complication Swatch introduced in November 2024 and which remains exclusive to the MoonSwatch: an earthphase indicator. Where a conventional moonphase shows the phases of the Moon as viewed from Earth, this does the reverse. The two displays run in opposite directions over the same 29.5-day cycle.

(How useful this is to those of us who live on Earth is debatable, but it’s one of the more original ideas in watchmaking in recent years.)

There are some nice details elsewhere on the 42mm Bioceramic watch, including Snoopy and Woodstock in the snow, a pair of miniature skis at 11 o’clock, and a tiny patch of white on the Earth representing a snow-covered Switzerland, which glows green under UV light.

As with last year’s winter edition, there’s a stipulation.

The watch goes on sale at selected Swatch stores worldwide on Black Friday, November 27. After that, until March 20, 2027, the last day of winter, you’ll only be able to buy one when it’s snowing in Switzerland.

Swatch will be providing weather updates on its website.

#6796. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

#6795. The 6 Best Gaming Headsets for All Types of Players

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re playing single-player or online, these will level up your game.

Nothing truly makes me feel immersed in a game the same way good sound does. And it’s impossible to get that level of tension in a horror game like Resident Evil, or the feeling of being absolutely locked in a competitive shooter without a good pair of headphones. Nowadays, video games are fully open world of 3D and spatial audio, and there's never been a better time to hop on the bandwagon. It's time to get a gaming headset.

If you consider gaming your main hobby, you’re probably either a single-player person or you like to squad up in your battle royale of choice. It doesn’t matter, actually, because the only proper way to experience both ends of the gaming spectrum is with a headset. For single-player, that means fully immersive audio. For multiplayer, you’ll sound crystal clear to your teammates—no more confusion or excuses. The good noise-canceling headsets even have transparency modes so you don’t have to ignore everyone in your real life either.

Here's the problem. There are actually plenty of good headsets and trustworthy brands that make dozens of similar options. Finding the headset that will work for you isn’t made to be easy, but that’s why I’m here. I know my gaming headsets like the back of my hand—that’s my job. To figure out if you need a high-end wireless SteelSeries headset, or if you’ll be happy with a sub-$100 option, read on. Hell, maybe you’d prefer earbuds. I’ve accounted for all options.

This is the one gaming headset I won’t (and frankly can’t) say anything negative about. I suppose that’s what $600 gets you. SteelSeries has long made comfortable headsets that support multiple platforms with easy swapping. It introduced the world to its GameHub base station, letting you connect all your systems to one panel and easily control them all from there.

The Nova Elite takes every already perfectly solved puzzle piece and puts a little extra sheen on it. First off, it is gorgeous, with exteriors made from premium steel, aluminum, and vegan leather. Nothing else in the gaming space compares to it in terms of the luxe look. In terms of fit, the Nova Elite distributes its weight evenly so you can play for hours without discomfort.

And this headset’s gaming hub has an extra feature that really changes things. The hot-swappable batteries are SteelSeries’ biggest innovation in recent years, and here the hub also acts as a charger for your second (included) headset battery. So whichever one is not powering your current session is being charged. What this means is battery life is a solved problem. You literally never have to worry about running out of charge mid-game again. And for some, that alone might be worth the price of admission.

Okay, forget what I just said. This is everything a wireless headset needs to be for the average gamer. ASUS has targeted a lower price point and succeeded in terms of knowing which features to cut, and what to keep to make it still feel like a high-quality experience. You don't get active noise cancellation or the ability to play audio from two sources at once. For those luxuries, go for the Delta II.

At 0.7 pounds, the Pelta is remarkably light and the furthest thing from bulky. It's about letting you play all day on those summer Saturdays where it's too hot to go outside. Where it surprises in this regard is battery life. Offering up to 70 hours between charges and a fit that won't leave you itchy and sweaty, this is the all-nighter-friendly headset.

While I wouldn't personally spring for a wired headset, those who do are hard to convince otherwise. And that crowd has spent the last couple of years rallying around the HyperX Cloud Alpha. If competitive gaming is the reason you are turning to a headset, this has everything you need for under $100.

You aren't going to get the audio options you do from the multi-hundred-dollar headsets SteelSeries and Corsair sell. Instead, you are paying a reasonable price for a high-quality microphone, and a comfortable pair of headphones. Stop worrying about lag and stop worrying about your headset running out of battery. It's time to wire in.

When it comes to sound quality, the Corsair Virtuoso Max brings it to a new level. This is a better-sounding headset than most of the readers of this article have ever experienced.

My favorite high-tech touch that we actually use is the simultaneous connection. Using the 2.4 GHz dongle and Bluetooth in tandem you can get the audio from your game and audio from a Bluetooth source at the same time. That means pumping in your own tunes or a background podcast while grinding levels. When playing with friends, I love the addition of a physical, sturdy, functional mic mute button.

The only issue is long-term comfort. Weighing in at just under a pound, the 60-hour battery life on the headset is a bit of a moot point. You'll want to take it off anyway because it can start to feel quite hard on your skull after a couple hours of white knuckle gaming.

If you have a single gaming machine and that console is a PS5, this is the headset I recommend. It is remarkably lightweight, weighing even less than the wired-only pair I recommended earlier, and it’s super easy to use—with a satisfying mute button that pops in and out, and a microphone that is entirely detachable. So if you’re settling in for a night of solo horror gaming, you can set the mic aside and still enjoy the excellent sound and spatial audio options. And if you know that battery is low, or you really just want the easiest solution, the package includes a 3.5mm cable for wired connection. Just plug that right into your DualSense controller and you’re off to the races.

Beyond just PlayStation diehards, this is an excellent option for any PC or Nintendo gamer who wants a light wireless headset. It is fully compatible via USB-C transceiver or headphone cable with any other modern console, sans the Xbox.

Okay, so I lied. When you clicked on this for the six best headsets, yeah one of these isn't a gaming headset at all. It's a set of earbuds designed by SteelSeries to be used on gaming consoles and it's as much of a game-changer as it sounds. If you feel cheated, don't fret. There are two more headsets listed in the honorable mentions below. Please accept my humble offering.

If you’ll allow me to talk about them for just a minute though, the Arctis GameBuds are just shy of a miracle. They are comfy earbuds with all-encompassing noise-canceling (gasp!), spatial audio support, and a very useful transparency/talk-through mode. The sound isn't quite on par with SteelSeries's larger headsets, but it still impresses. Plus, unlike the rest of these headsets, you can fit them in your pocket.

SteelSeries Arctis Nova 7 ($179): You don't need to shell out for the Nova Elite to get a good Arctis headset. This one uses a simple USB-C dongle, so isn't nearly as complicated to set up, either.

Turtle Beach Stealth Pro 2: ($349): My favorite new headset of the year. An excellent multi-platform choice with high-quality playback and call audio. It also uses a swappable battery system, so you never run out of charge in a heated gaming moment.

Testing gaming headsets really isn't too difficult. You make sure it's compatible with what it says on the box, and then tell the people if it’s comfortable and if it sounds good. Shit simple until you do it enough to realize a simple truth. Most models from reputable brands are worth their costs, and very few companies have duds in their lineups. That said, there are ways to tell what's going to rise from the crop. Here's some pointers for the next time you shop for headsets.

Comfort — If you can't stay comfortable in them for more than an hour they are going straight in the trash. Even the most uncomfortable among these picks are still in the top 5% of comfortable gaming headphones.

Sound Quality — Like with the pair of headphones you use to listen to music, you want clear sound with plenty of range.

Microphone — If you're gaming online, your microphone needs to cut out unwanted background noise. Some offer noise cancelling, some just have good unidirectional builds. All on this list are great in this department.

Directional Sound — Spatial audio is cool when watching movies, but when playing games this can take your team play to the next level. When you hear an enemy's footsteps, you can know they're behind you at four o'clock, not six. And that makes you an asset. It also makes the taste of victory that much sweeter.

Connection — I will discuss wireless vs. wired shortly, but when it comes to the going wireless there's usually two ways to connect a gaming headset. Bluetooth or via a USB dongle. The latter typically has less latency and is the preferred way to connect.

Are wired or wireless headsets better for gaming?

If you care about latency (aka lag), we are going to recommend wired over wireless. The added hassle of wireless charging and connecting doesn't necessarily make up for the hassle of having a wire. Plus, you don't have to worry about it not working on some devices—which you do with pretty much every wireless device you buy.

Do all gaming headsets work across all platforms?

I'm glad you asked. No! As you may have noticed, there tend to be two kinds of headsets. Those that work with Xbox.. and those that work with everything else. Since Xbox uses its own wireless standard instead of Bluetooth. In practice, this means Xbox gamers need to be extra careful when buying headsets to make sure the one they're getting is compatible. SteelSeries, for example, sells two versions of each headset, one marked for Xbox.

It's actually more complicated than "all headsets that work with PC also work with PlayStation and you have to get ones especially for Xbox," but in many of these cases that is the gist. Although the PlayStation headsets work on PC and Mac but not Nintendo Switch. So, yeah, it's pretty inconsistent. Seriously, just read the list of compatible platforms before you buy a headset.

#6789. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.

#6784. Meet the Cast and Characters of ‘Blade Runner 2099’

The ’80s film Blade Runner is the latest to get the streaming series treatment, after the legacy sequel Blade Runner 2049 revitalized the science fiction noir franchise about humanoid robots called “replicants” that slowly took over society back in 2017. The new series takes place 80 years after that original film. What’s changed? Who’s in charge now? Before you press play on Blade Runner 2099, let’s run through the cast and characters.

This is the official description of the series from Prime Video:

Which of the new characters are human? Which ones are replicants? Does it even matter anymore? Here’s what you need to know.

Olwen is an old-school replicant Blade Runner on the verge of retirement.

Michelle Yeoh is a former pageant queen, ballet dancer, and Academy Award-winning actress. You know her from Everything Everywhere All At Once, Wicked, Crazy Rich Asians, Memoirs of a Geisha, Shang-Chi and the Legend of the Ten Rings, Star Trek: Discovery, American Born Chinese, and Crouching Tiger, Hidden Dragon. She is also a Golden Globe, Critics Choice, and Actor Award winner. Before all of that, she had a prolific Hong Kong action and martial arts filmography. Legend!

Cora is a replicant who goes undercover as a human.

Hunter Schafer is a model and actress best known for playing Jules on Euphoria. She played President Snow’s cousin Tigris in The Hunger Games: The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes and followed that up with the films Cuckoo, Kinds of Kindness, and Mother Mary. Next, you’ll see her in the upcoming Tom Ford movie Cry to Heaven, adapted from Anne Rice’s historical novel.

Bebeto is a human who teams up with Cora.

Lewis starred as Danny in the drama series Somewhere Boy. You might also recognize him from Masters of the Air, Generation Z, Shetland, and Silent Witness. He played a young version of Peter Capaldi’s character in the Black Mirror episode “Plaything.” His film credits include gnarly horror movies like The Severed Sun and The Damned. Next you’ll see him in the ensemble cast of Lorna Tucker’s Bare.

Matthew is best known for playing Larys Strong on House of the Dragon. (His co-star Freddie Fox also appears in the Blade Runner 2099 pilot.) He also played Lucien Bonaparte in Ridley Scott’s film Napoleon, and starred in the medical drama series Casualty. If you’re a historical drama junkie, you might know him as Mr. Crowe from Sanditon and/or Mr. Drummie from the 2023 Great Expectations.

Helena is a very mysterious character you’ll glimpse in the first episode.

Sheila Atim is an Olivier Award-winning actress for her supporting role in the Bob Dylan jukebox musical Girl from the North Country. You might recognize her from films and shows like Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, The Woman King, Harlots, and The Underground Railroad. His other film credits include The Souvenir, The Souvenir Part II, Living, The Wonder, and Black Bag. Some of his television credits include Jerico, The Hour, and classic literary adaptations like War & Peace, Great Expectations, and The Musketeers. You’ll see him next in Robert Eggers’ Werwulf and the upcoming Elden Ring movie.

Daniel is a BAFTA-winning actor for the television film Eric and Ernie. You might recognize him from Flowers, Jericho, Watership Down, Sick Note, and Renegade Nell. He also does a lot of stage acting, including a one-man show, and previously worked as a stand-up comedian.

Amy Lennox is a prolific West End theater actress who was nominated for an Olivier Award in 2016. She played Chloe Godard on 100 episodes of the medical drama Holby City and also starred in the short-lived sitcom Only Child.

You’ll meet this mysterious character in the first episode.

Katelyn is an Irish actress you may have seen before in The Princess with Joey King, the murder mystery series Clean Sweep, or The Nun II.

#6783. 13 Pairs of Gloves to Keep Your Digits Toasty All Winter Long

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Don’t overlook your cold-weather style practicalities.

There are few winter experiences more miserable than stepping outside with bare hands and realizing, too late, that your fingers have stopped working. First they’re freezing, then they’re numb, and before long your skin feels dry and rough and beyond the help of any hand lotion.

But honestly? Gloves can be annoying, too. Some turn a quick phone check into a whole production; others are so bulky you might as well be wearing oven mitts. The best winter gloves keep your hands warm without making your fingers useless—and look good while they’re at it. We’ve rounded up our favorites, from waterproof, windproof pairs built for rough weather to stylish options that still keep your hands nice and toasty. And yes, plenty have touchscreen-compatible fingertips so you can still call and text (and play the Wordle). See our favorite pairs below.

These luxurious leather gloves come from Overland, the brand behind some of our favorite coats. The smooth leather looks very polished, so these gloves can be dressed up if you want, but they go just as well with a casual outfit. They’re also quite durable and are naturally water-repellant, and the cashmere lining adds warmth on the inside.

Commerce Editor Luke Guillory loves these merino wool-lined deerskin gloves from Geier Gloves, a brand that has been making leather products in the U.S. for almost a century. Since the brand specializes in leather, you can bet the deerskin on these gloves is of the highest quality, and the wool lining is very nice, too. Geier also makes a lot of wor, driving, and motorcycle gloves, so you know they’re the real deal. The black and brown colorways look quite polished, but the natural and saddle leather options are also great.

If you want a nice luxurious pair of cashmere gloves, go with these ones from Sunspel. Lately, I’ve been wearing these gloves, which were knitted in Scotland from super soft pure cashmere. The brand has been perfecting the craft for over 150 years, so you can trust that these are the best of the best. It’s basically like enveloping your hands in a cozy blanket. For a full set, get the brand’s cashmere hat and scarf.

These Canada Goose gloves are basically the equivalent of wearing a puffer jacket on your hands. With heavy-duty, long rib-knit cuffs for extra protection and 750-fill-power down on the inside, your hands will stay warm and dry without their mobility being super impacted by something extra bulky. Note that these have a touchscreen-compatible index finger but not thumb.

Wool-blend gloves are as handsome as any of the very best sweaters in your rotation. Even though the outside is made from knit material, the insulated interior will keep your hands warm and dry during your next epic snowball fight. They’re also thin enough to layer under another pair of gloves if it’s really freezing outside.

When it comes to gloves, there’s one brand I’ve trusted for years, and that’s Smartwool. Their gloves are warm without being bulky, and mine have lasted for years. Having a warm pair of gloves that actually does its job well is even more important for my boyfriend, who has Raynaud’s and needs extra protection for his hands, and the gloves he relies on year after year? Also Smartwool. These new fleece gloves from the brand have extra insulation on the back to provide serious warmth, and they’re also functional with touchscreen-compatible pads on the thumbs and index fingers. Sure, they’re a little expensive for fleece gloves at $68—but if you don’t need the extra insulation, you can try the regular Active Fleece Gloves for $48.

If all you need is a solid pair of knit gloves and you’re not looking to spend a fortune, look no further than Uniqlo. The brand’s Heattech knit gloves are soft and warm and have effective touchscreen-compatible fingertips. They also come in 11 fun colors, so you might as well get a couple pairs for yourself—and they make a great holiday gift for literally anyone.

You already know we love Barbour’s waxed canvas jackets, so it’s no surprise we’re also a fan of the brand’s stylish, preppy gloves. These leather-palmed gloves have a cotton tartan face for a cool contrast and wool lining on the inside for extra warmth—and they’re waterproof, so you can break them out no matter what’s going on with the weather. Pair these with the Barbour Beaufort jacket for an iconic look.

Knit gloves are great when it comes to keeping warm, but they’re not the best when it’s raining or snowing. No one wants a pair of soaking gloves (that probably make your hands feel even colder). Enter these waterproof gloves from the brand that brought you the famous Bean Boots. With a waterproof nylon exterior and a down fill, they’ll keep rain and show out while keeping your hands warm. Note that some say the fingers are long, so consider that when choosing a size.

When you’re stuck in a blizzard or your location is prone to unpredictable weather, you’ll need something a little more heavy duty. REI’s weatherproof gore-tex gloves will give your hands the protection they need. They’re also essentially a two-in-one: The removable fleece liners can be used on their own or with the weatherproof outers as an extra layer of warmth.

Naadam’s famous cashmere sweater is one of our favorite pieces of winter clothing and one of our go-to gifts for the holidays, birthdays, Valentine’s Day, etc. The brand makes a matching pair of soft cashmere gloves, which come in 13 colors and are under $100. A genius gift idea? Get them the sweater with a matching pair of gloves, and they’ll wear both all winter long.

If you want that classic Sherlock Holmes leather glove look, you have lots of choices. But if you don’t want to spend a ton on a pair, get these $60 gloves from Quince. Not only do they have a nice leather exterior in either brown or black, they also have a cashmere lining on the inside.

On the high end of the high end, Gucci has a gorgeous luxury pair of leather gloves with the brand’s signature horsebit on the wrist for a fun little detail. Made from leather and silk, these are soft, smooth, and very stylish.

#6782. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

#6772. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

AI agents have moved into mainstream enterprise use, but the content infrastructure needed to support them has struggled to keep up, according to a new report from cloud content management company Box.
